Minimize f(x) = ½‖x − b‖₂² over the probability simplex Δ = {x ∈ ℝⁿ | x ≥ 0, Σ xᵢ = 1} with b = (0.6, 0.2, 0.2)ᵀ. Perform two iterations of Frank‑Wolfe starting from x₀ = (1/3, 1/3, 1/3)ᵀ using step size γₖ = 2/(k+2).
Iteration 0: ∇f(x₀) = x₀ − b = (−0.267, −0.133, −0.133)ᵀ. LMO: minimize ∇f(x₀)ᵀs over Δ → choose vertex e₁ = (1,0,0)ᵀ because its component is most negative. γ₀ = 2/2 = 1. x₁ = x₀ + 1·(e₁ − x₀) = e₁ = (1,0,0)ᵀ. Iteration 1: ∇f(x₁) = x₁ − b = (0.4, −0.2, −0.2)ᵀ. LMO: most negative component is −0.2 (indices 2 or 3); pick e₂ = (0,1,0)ᵀ. γ₁ = 2/3 ≈ 0.667. x₂ = x₁ + γ₁(e₂ − x₁) = (1,0,0) + 0.667·(−1,1,0) = (0.333, 0.667, 0)ᵀ. The objective values: f(x₀)=0.089, f(x₁)=0.18, f(x₂)=0.056. The method moves toward the projection of b onto Δ, which is b itself since b∈Δ.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the convergence rate of Frank‑Wolfe, recall the O(1/k) rate for general convex smooth functions and linear convergence only under strong convexity plus a polytope constraint with away steps. Identify whether the constraint set admits a cheap LMO; if the problem involves a nuclear norm ball or a flow polytope, Frank‑Wolfe is the method of choice. For step‑size rules, the open‑loop γₖ = 2/(k+2) is standard; line‑search variants are rarely required in competitive exams.

Stochastic Variance‑Reduced Gradient Methods for Finite‑Sum Problems

Finite‑sum optimization minₓ (1/n) Σᵢ₌₁ⁿ fᵢ(x) arises in empirical risk minimization. Stochastic gradient descent (SGD) uses an unbiased estimator ∇fᵢ(x) with variance that prevents linear convergence. Variance‑reduced methods construct a control variate to reduce variance while keeping per‑iteration cost low. SVRG (Stochastic Variance Reduced Gradient) maintains a snapshot point x̃ updated every m iterations. The gradient estimator is vₖ = ∇fᵢₖ(xₖ) − ∇fᵢₖ(x̃) + (1/n) Σᵢ ∇fᵢ(x̃). The full gradient at x̃ is computed once per epoch (cost O(n)), and inner iterations use only two component gradients. With step size η = 1/(10L) and epoch length m = O(n), SVRG achieves a linear convergence rate E[f(xₖ) − f*] ≤ ρᵏ (f(x₀) − f*) for μ‑strongly convex and L‑smooth fᵢ, where ρ < 1 depends on condition number κ = L/μ. SARAH (Stochastic Recursive Gradient) uses a recursive estimator vₖ = ∇fᵢₖ(xₖ) − ∇fᵢₖ(xₖ₋₁) + vₖ₋₁ with occasional full‑gradient resets, yielding similar linear rates with a smaller constant. SAGA stores a table of past gradients φᵢ ≈ ∇fᵢ(φᵢ) and updates vₖ = ∇fᵢₖ(xₖ) − φᵢₖ + (1/n) Σᵢ φᵢ, achieving linear convergence without full‑gradient epochs. For non‑strongly convex problems, the rates become sublinear O(1/k) but with better constants than SGD. Practical considerations: the memory requirement of SAGA (O(nd)) can be prohibitive for high‑dimensional data; SVRG and SARAH need only O(d) memory. Mini‑batch variants reduce variance further and improve parallelism. In distributed settings, D‑SVRG and D‑SARAH communicate the snapshot gradient across workers. These methods are the backbone of modern large‑scale machine learning libraries and are essential for training models where n is in the millions.

Branch‑and‑Price Algorithms for Large‑Scale Vehicle Routing

Vehicle routing problems (VRP) with hundreds of customers are modeled as set partitioning formulations: min Σⱼ cⱼ λⱼ s.t. Σⱼ aᵢⱼ λⱼ = 1 ∀i, λⱼ ∈ {0,1}, where each column j represents a feasible route with cost cⱼ and aᵢⱼ = 1 if route j visits customer i. The number of columns is exponential, so column generation solves the LP relaxation by iteratively adding columns with negative reduced cost. The pricing problem is a resource‑constrained shortest path (RCSP) on a graph with time‑window, capacity, or duration constraints; it is solved by dynamic programming with label‑setting algorithms. Branch‑and‑price integrates column generation inside a branch‑and‑bound tree: at each node the LP relaxation is solved by column generation, and branching decisions are made on fractional variables. Common branching rules include branching on arc flow variables (xₑ = Σⱼ aₑⱼ λⱼ) to preserve the RCSP structure, or Ryan‑Foster branching on pairs of customers that are either always together or never together in a route. Strong branching evaluates the impact of candidate branches by solving the LP relaxation with the branch imposed. Cutting planes such as capacity cuts, 2‑path cuts, and subset‑row cuts are added to strengthen the LP bound (branch‑and‑price‑and‑cut). Heuristic pricing (e.g., limited discrepancy search) speeds up column generation early in the tree. The algorithm terminates when all leaf nodes are fathomed by integrality or bound. State‑of‑the‑art solvers (e.g., VRPSolver, SCIP with VRP plugins) combine these techniques to solve instances with up to 1000 customers. Understanding the interaction between branching and pricing is crucial: branching on arc flows keeps the pricing problem a standard RCSP, while branching on λ variables directly destroys the network structure and makes pricing NP‑hard.

Distributionally Robust Optimization with φ‑Divergence Ambiguity Sets

Distributionally robust optimization (DRO) hedges against ambiguity in the probability distribution of uncertain parameters. A φ‑divergence ambiguity set is defined as 𝒫 = {Q ≪ P₀ | D_φ(Q‖P₀) ≤ ρ}, where P₀ is a reference (empirical) distribution, D_φ(Q‖P₀) = Σᵢ p₀ᵢ φ(qᵢ/p₀ᵢ) for discrete distributions, φ is a convex function with φ(1)=0, and ρ ≥ 0 controls the size. Common choices: Kullback‑Leibler (KL) divergence φ(t)=t log t, χ²‑divergence φ(t)=(t−1)², and Cressie‑Read family φ(t)=(t^γ−γ(t−1)−1)/(γ(γ−1)). The DRO problem minₓ supQ∈𝒫 E_Q[ℓ(x,ξ)] can be reformulated using convex duality. For a fixed x, the inner supremum is infλ≥0, η { λρ + η + λ Σᵢ p₀ᵢ φ*((ℓ(x,ξᵢ)−η)/λ) }, where φ* is the convex conjugate of φ. This yields a tractable convex program when ℓ is convex in x and the conjugate φ* is computable. For KL divergence, φ*(s)=es−1, leading to an exponential cone program; for χ², φ*(s)=s²/4 + s, giving a second‑order cone program. The radius ρ is often calibrated from statistical confidence bounds: with probability 1−β, the true distribution lies in 𝒫 if ρ = (1/N) χ²1−β(m) for χ²‑divergence, where m is the support size. DRO with φ‑divergence provides a bridge between robust optimization (ρ→∞) and stochastic programming (ρ→0). It is particularly useful when the empirical distribution has limited samples, as it avoids overfitting to the sample while retaining tractability. Recent work extends φ‑divergence DRO to continuous distributions via kernel embeddings and to multi‑stage problems using dynamic programming with ambiguity sets at each stage.

  • Confusing the convergence rate of Frank‑Wolfe (O(1/k)) with that of accelerated gradient (O(1/k²)) for smooth convex problems.
  • Assuming SVRG requires a full gradient at every iteration; the full gradient is computed only once per epoch.
  • Branching on λ variables in branch‑and‑price destroys the network structure of the pricing problem, making it NP‑hard.
  • Using a φ‑divergence radius ρ that is too small, which yields a solution indistinguishable from the nominal stochastic program and loses robustness.
  • Neglecting to verify that the loss function ℓ(x,ξ) is convex in x before applying the dual reformulation of φ‑divergence DRO.
  • Overlooking the need for a self‑concordant barrier when implementing a custom interior‑point method for the exponential cone arising from KL‑divergence DRO.
  • Treating the away‑step Frank‑Wolfe direction as a simple gradient step and ignoring the active‑set maintenance required for linear convergence.

Scenario Generation and Reduction for Multi‑Stage Stochastic Programming

Multi‑stage stochastic programs require a scenario tree that captures the evolution of uncertain parameters over time. The quality of the tree directly affects solution accuracy and computational burden. A common construction pipeline starts with a large set of sample paths generated by Monte Carlo simulation or quasi‑Monte Carlo sequences (Sobol, Halton). Each path is a realization of the stochastic process across all stages. To obtain a tractable tree, these paths are clustered stage‑wise using algorithms such as k‑means on the conditional distributions or hierarchical agglomerative clustering with a distance metric that respects the filtration (e.g., the nested distance). Moment‑matching techniques then adjust the probabilities of the cluster centroids so that the first few moments (mean, covariance, skewness) of the reduced tree match those of the original sample set.

Scenario reduction aims to discard redundant scenarios while preserving the stochastic program’s optimal value within a prescribed tolerance. The classic fast forward selection algorithm iteratively adds the scenario that maximizes the reduction in a chosen discrepancy measure (often the Kantorovich or Wasserstein distance) until a target cardinality is reached. Backward reduction starts from the full set and removes the scenario whose deletion causes the smallest increase in the discrepancy. Both methods can be accelerated by exploiting the tree structure: at each stage, only scenarios sharing the same history need be compared. The Wasserstein distance between the original and reduced discrete distributions provides a rigorous bound on the optimal value gap: if the distance is ε, the optimal value of the reduced problem deviates by at most L·ε, where L is the Lipschitz constant of the recourse function. In practice, a two‑phase approach works well: first generate a moderately large tree (e.g., 10⁴–10⁵ leaf nodes) using importance sampling to oversample tail events, then apply backward reduction to reach a few hundred scenarios that still capture extreme outcomes. Stability analysis — re‑solving the problem on several independently reduced trees — quantifies the sensitivity of decisions to scenario selection and guards against overfitting to a particular sample.

Progressive Hedging Algorithm for Stochastic Programming

Progressive Hedging (PH) decomposes a multi‑stage stochastic program by scenario, solving independent scenario subproblems and coordinating them through a quadratic penalty on non‑anticipativity violations. For a two‑stage problem with scenarios s = 1,…,S, the algorithm maintains scenario‑specific first‑stage decisions xˢ and a consensus vector x̄. At iteration k, each subproblem minimizes its local objective plus (ρ/2)‖xˢ − x̄ᵏ + uˢᵏ‖², where ρ > 0 is the penalty parameter and uˢ is the scaled dual variable. After solving all subproblems (often in parallel), the consensus is updated as x̄ᵏ⁺¹ = Σₛ pₛ xˢᵏ⁺¹, and the dual variables are updated via uˢᵏ⁺¹ = uˢᵏ + xˢᵏ⁺¹ − x̄ᵏ⁺¹. Convergence is guaranteed for convex problems when ρ is fixed and the subproblems are solved exactly; for non‑convex or integer subproblems, heuristic variants (e.g., progressive hedging with branch‑and‑bound) are used. The choice of ρ critically affects speed: too small yields slow consensus, too large causes ill‑conditioned subproblems. Adaptive schemes increase ρ when the primal residual ‖xˢ − x̄‖ stagnates and decrease it when the dual residual ‖x̄ᵏ⁺¹ − x̄ᵏ‖ dominates. Stopping criteria combine primal and dual residual norms scaled by problem data (e.g., εₚᵣᵢ = √S·εₐᵦₛ + εᵣₑₗ·max{‖xˢ‖,‖x̄‖}). PH extends naturally to multi‑stage problems by enforcing non‑anticipativity at each stage separately, leading to a block‑diagonal penalty structure that preserves the scenario‑wise separability. The method is closely related to ADMM; indeed, PH applied to the extensive form with consensus constraints is equivalent to ADMM on the dual problem. This equivalence allows borrowing convergence acceleration techniques such as over‑relaxation (α ∈ (1,2)) and residual balancing.

Progressive Hedging Parameter Tuning
A two‑stage stochastic linear program has 50 scenarios with equal probability. The first‑stage variable dimension is 20. After 10 PH iterations with ρ = 1, the primal residual norm is 0.45 and the dual residual norm is 0.08. The algorithm appears to be converging slowly. Propose a systematic adjustment of ρ and justify it using the residual balancing principle.
Residual balancing aims to keep primal and dual residuals within a constant factor of each other. Here the primal residual (0.45) is roughly 5.6 times the dual residual (0.08). According to the standard heuristic, if ‖rᵖʳⁱ‖ > μ‖rᵈᵘᵃˡ‖ with μ = 10, increase ρ by a factor τ > 1 (commonly τ = 2). Since 0.45 > 10·0.08 = 0.8 is false, the condition is not met; however, the ratio 5.6 suggests the primal residual is still dominant. A practical rule is to increase ρ when the ratio exceeds 2. Thus set ρ ← 2·ρ = 2. Re‑run a few iterations; if the primal residual drops faster than the dual residual grows, the new ρ is effective. If the dual residual becomes dominant (ratio < 0.5), halve ρ. This adaptive scheme typically reduces iteration count by 30–50 % compared with a fixed ρ.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks to choose a decomposition method, check the problem structure: block‑angular constraints with linking variables → Benders or Dantzig‑Wolfe; scenario‑wise separable objective with non‑anticipativity constraints → Progressive Hedging or ADMM. Remember that PH requires convex subproblems for guaranteed convergence; if integer variables appear only in the first stage, PH can still be applied with a branch‑and‑bound wrapper.

Proximal Gradient Methods and Operator Splitting in Convex Optimization

Proximal gradient methods solve composite problems of the form minimize f(x) + g(x) where f is smooth convex with Lipschitz gradient (constant L) and g is possibly non‑smooth but proximable (i.e., its proximal operator proxγg(v) = argminₓ { g(x) + (1/(2γ))‖x − v‖² } is computable in closed form or via a cheap subroutine). The basic iteration is xk+1 = proxγg(x^k − γ∇f(x^k)) with step size γ ∈ (0, 2/L). This is the Iterative Shrinkage‑Thresholding Algorithm (ISTA) with convergence rate O(1/k) in objective value. Nesterov’s acceleration yields FISTA: y^k = x^k + ((tk-1−1)/t_k)(x^k − xk-1), xk+1 = proxγg(y^k − γ∇f(y^k)), tk+1 = (1+√(1+4t_k²))/2, achieving O(1/k²). The proximal operator appears in many regularizers: ℓ₁ norm gives soft‑thresholding, nuclear norm gives singular‑value thresholding, indicator of a convex set gives Euclidean projection. Operator splitting generalizes this idea to sums of multiple non‑smooth terms or to problems with linear constraints. Douglas‑Rachford splitting solves minimize f(x) + g(x) by iterating on the reflected proximal maps: zk+1 = (1/2)(I + RγgRγf)z^k, xk+1 = proxγf(zk+1), where Rγh = 2proxγh − I. ADMM is a special case of Douglas‑Rachford applied to the dual of a linearly constrained problem. Convergence of these splittings is guaranteed for proper closed convex functions; linear convergence occurs under strong convexity or error‑bound conditions. Practical implementations often incorporate adaptive step‑size selection (e.g., backtracking line search on the gradient mapping) and restart schemes for accelerated variants to mitigate the “oscillation” phenomenon. These methods underpin modern large‑scale solvers for Lasso, matrix completion, total variation denoising, and sparse inverse covariance estimation.

Sequential Quadratic Programming for Nonlinear Programming

Sequential Quadratic Programming (SQP) is the workhorse for solving smooth nonlinear programs (NLPs) with equality and inequality constraints. At each iteration, given a current iterate x^k and Lagrange multiplier estimates λ^k, μ^k, SQP constructs a quadratic programming (QP) subproblem that approximates the Lagrangian L(x,λ,μ) = f(x) + λᵀc_E(x) + μᵀc_I(x) by a quadratic model using the Hessian of the Lagrangian (or a quasi‑Newton approximation such as BFGS) and linearizes the constraints: minimize ∇f(x^k)ᵀd + ½ dᵀB^k d subject to c_E(x^k) + ∇c_E(x^k)ᵀd = 0, c_I(x^k) + ∇c_I(x^k)ᵀd ≤ 0. The step d^k is then used to update xk+1 = x^k + α^k d^k, where the step length α^k is determined by a merit function (e.g., augmented Lagrangian or ℓ₁ exact penalty) to ensure global convergence. Trust‑region SQP variants replace the line search with a trust‑region radius Δ^k, solving a constrained QP that limits ‖d‖ ≤ Δ^k; the radius is adjusted based on the ratio of actual to predicted reduction. The Hessian approximation B^k is critical: exact Hessians provide fast local convergence but are expensive; limited‑memory BFGS (L‑BFGS) offers a good trade‑off for large‑scale problems. Constraint qualifications (LICQ, MFCQ) guarantee that the QP subproblem is consistent and that KKT conditions of the NLP are necessary for optimality. Modern SQP implementations (e.g., SNOPT, IPOPT’s SQP mode, KNITRO) incorporate sophisticated features: elastic mode for infeasible QPs, second‑order corrections to avoid the Maratos effect, and inertia‑controlling factorizations for indefinite Hessians. Convergence theory shows that, under standard assumptions, SQP achieves superlinear convergence when the Hessian is exact and the step is accepted with α = 1 near the solution. For problems with expensive function evaluations, derivative‑free SQP variants use interpolation models to approximate gradients and Hessians.

Evolutionary Multi‑Objective Optimization: NSGA‑II and MOEA/D

Evolutionary algorithms (EAs) approximate the Pareto front of multi‑objective problems without requiring gradient information or convexity assumptions. NSGA‑II (Non‑Dominated Sorting Genetic Algorithm II) maintains a population of size N and proceeds in generations. Each generation combines parent and offspring populations (size 2N), performs fast non‑dominated sorting to assign rank (front) numbers, and computes crowding distance within each front to preserve diversity. Selection for the next generation fills fronts in order of increasing rank; if a front exceeds the remaining slots, individuals with largest crowding distance are chosen. Crossover (simulated binary crossover) and mutation (polynomial mutation) generate offspring. The algorithm’s complexity is O(MN²) per generation for M objectives, dominated by the sorting step. MOEA/D (Multi‑Objective Evolutionary Algorithm based on Decomposition) takes a different approach: it decomposes the multi‑objective problem into a set of scalar subproblems using weight vectors λ¹,…,λᴺ (e.g., Tchebycheff or weighted sum). Each subproblem i minimizes g^te(x|λⁱ) = max1≤j≤M λ_jⁱ |f_j(x) − z_j^*|, where z^* is the ideal point. Neighborhoods are defined by Euclidean distance between weight vectors; mating and replacement are restricted to the neighborhood, promoting local search. MOEA/D’s complexity is O(N·T) where T is neighborhood size, typically much smaller than N. Both algorithms have been extended with adaptive operator selection, reference‑point based selection (NSGA‑III), and dynamic resource allocation. Performance metrics for comparing Pareto front approximations include the hypervolume indicator (volume dominated by the front relative to a reference point), the inverted generational distance (IGD), and the spacing metric. In practice, NSGA‑II excels when the front is convex and well‑spread, while MOEA/D often outperforms on discontinuous or degenerate fronts due to its decomposition flexibility. Hybridizations that inject local gradient‑based search (memetic EAs) or surrogate models (Kriging, radial basis functions) further improve sample efficiency for expensive black‑box objectives.

  • Confusing the convergence rate of ISTA (O(1/k)) with FISTA (O(1/k²)) and applying the wrong step‑size rule.
  • Assuming Progressive Hedging converges for non‑convex integer subproblems without a branch‑and‑bound wrapper.
  • Using a fixed penalty parameter ρ in PH when the primal‑dual residual ratio exceeds 10, leading to stagnation.
  • Neglecting to verify the Mangasarian‑Fromovitz constraint qualification before trusting SQP’s KKT multipliers.
  • Applying NSGA‑II’s crowding distance in high‑dimensional objective spaces (M > 5) where it loses discriminative power.
  • Treating the proximal operator of a non‑convex regularizer as if it were single‑valued, causing algorithmic divergence.
  • Review these traps before the exam to avoid common mistakes.

Lagrangian Decomposition and Dual Ascent for Large‑Scale Integer Programs

Lagrangian decomposition relaxes complicating constraints that couple otherwise independent substructures, assigning a multiplier vector to each relaxed constraint. The resulting Lagrangian dual provides a bound that is at least as strong as the linear programming relaxation when the subproblems are solved to optimality. In practice, the subproblems are often mixed‑integer programs that can be tackled by dedicated branch‑and‑bound solvers, while the dual is maximized by a subgradient method, bundle method, or accelerated proximal gradient scheme. A key design choice is the selection of which constraints to relax: typical candidates are linking constraints in multi‑period planning, assignment constraints in facility location, or flow conservation constraints in network design. The dual function is concave but non‑smooth; subgradient updates use step sizes that satisfy the diminishing‑step‑size rule or the Polyak step size when a target dual value is known. Bundle methods stabilize the ascent by maintaining a cutting‑plane model of the dual function, yielding faster convergence and a natural stopping criterion based on the duality gap. When the subproblems exhibit integrality properties (e.g., totally unimodular constraint matrices), the Lagrangian bound coincides with the LP bound, and the decomposition reduces to a pure dual ascent on the LP dual. For general MIPs, the bound is often tighter, but a primal feasible solution must be recovered. Heuristic primal recovery strategies include solving a restricted master problem with the best Lagrangian multipliers, applying a rounding scheme to the subproblem solutions, or using the multipliers to guide a local search. Convergence of the dual ascent to the optimal dual value is guaranteed under standard assumptions (bounded subgradients, appropriate step sizes), but the number of iterations can be large; therefore, hybrid schemes that alternate between dual ascent and a few branch‑and‑bound nodes on the original problem are common in commercial solvers.

Practical Implementation Tips

  • Scale the relaxed constraints so that multiplier magnitudes are comparable; this improves subgradient step‑size selection.
  • Warm‑start subproblem solves with the previous iteration’s solution to exploit similarity.
  • Monitor the duality gap; if it stalls, consider adding violated valid inequalities to the subproblems (Lagrangian cut generation).
  • Use a bundle method when the number of relaxed constraints exceeds a few hundred, as plain subgradient methods become erratic.

Benders Decomposition with Pareto‑Optimal Cuts and Multi‑Cut Variants

Classical Benders decomposition separates a mixed‑integer master problem from a linear (or convex) subproblem. The master proposes first‑stage decisions; the subproblem evaluates second‑stage recourse and generates optimality or feasibility cuts. A single cut per iteration can be weak, especially when the subproblem has multiple extreme dual solutions. Pareto‑optimal cuts dominate all other valid cuts for the same master solution: they are obtained by solving an auxiliary linear program that maximizes the violation of the cut subject to the dual feasible region and a normalization condition. This auxiliary LP is often small because it only involves the dual variables of the subproblem. Multi‑cut Benders generates a separate cut for each scenario (or each subproblem block) instead of aggregating them into one cut. The multi‑cut version yields a tighter master relaxation and often reduces the number of master iterations dramatically, at the cost of a larger master problem. A practical compromise is the partial multi‑cut approach, where scenarios are clustered and one cut per cluster is added. Magnanti‑Wong’s method for Pareto‑optimal cuts requires a core point in the relative interior of the master’s feasible region; this core point can be updated periodically by solving the LP relaxation of the master. When the subproblem is a network flow problem, the dual extreme points correspond to spanning tree solutions, and Pareto‑optimal cuts can be derived combinatorially without solving the auxiliary LP. For stochastic programs with integer recourse, the subproblem is a MIP; the classical Benders framework does not apply directly. Instead, one can use the integer L‑shaped method, which generates cuts from the LP relaxation of the subproblem and adds combinatorial cuts (e.g., no‑good cuts) to exclude infeasible integer second‑stage decisions. Recent advances combine Pareto‑optimal cuts with the integer L‑shaped method, yielding significant speed‑ups on capacitated facility location and stochastic vehicle routing instances.

Worked Example: Pareto‑Optimal Cut Generation
Consider a two‑stage stochastic program with first‑stage variable x ∈ {0,1} and second‑stage LP: minimize 3y₁ + 2y₂ subject to y₁ + y₂ ≥ 5 − 2x, y₁, y₂ ≥ 0. For x = 0, the subproblem dual is maximize (5)π subject to π ≤ 3, π ≤ 2, π ≥ 0. The dual optimal value is 10 with π = 2. Generate the Pareto‑optimal cut for this master solution using core point x̄ = 0.5.
The dual feasible region is π ∈ [0,2]. The core point x̄ = 0.5 gives right‑hand side 5 − 2·0.5 = 4. The auxiliary LP for Pareto‑optimal cut: maximize (4)π' subject to π' ≤ 3, π' ≤ 2, π' ≥ 0, and (5)π' = 10 (normalization to match optimal dual value at x=0). The normalization forces π' = 2. The resulting cut is θ ≥ 10 − 4x, where θ is the master’s estimate of second‑stage cost. This cut dominates the classical cut θ ≥ 10 − 4x (identical here because dual is unique). If the dual had multiple optima, the Pareto‑optimal cut would have a larger slope magnitude, cutting off more of the master’s feasible region.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks to compare single‑cut vs multi‑cut Benders, emphasize that multi‑cut provides a tighter master relaxation and fewer iterations but increases master size; mention Pareto‑optimal cuts as a way to strengthen single‑cut without increasing master dimension. For integer recourse, name the integer L‑shaped method and note that Pareto‑optimal cuts can be adapted via the LP relaxation of the subproblem.

Stochastic Programming with Coherent Risk Measures: CVaR and Optimized Certainty Equivalents

Risk‑averse stochastic programming replaces the expectation operator with a coherent risk measure ρ that satisfies monotonicity, translation invariance, positive homogeneity, and subadditivity. The most widely used is Conditional Value‑at‑Risk (CVaR) at confidence level α ∈ (0,1), defined as the expected loss in the worst (1−α) fraction of scenarios. CVaR can be linearized by introducing auxiliary variables η and z_s for each scenario s: ρ(X) = min_η { η + (1/(1−α)) Σ_s p_s z_s : z_s ≥ X_s − η, z_s ≥ 0 }. This formulation integrates seamlessly into two‑stage or multi‑stage stochastic linear programs, preserving linearity and enabling decomposition. Optimized Certainty Equivalents (OCE) generalize CVaR by using a convex disutility function u: ρ_u(X) = supη { η − E[u(η − X)] }. For piecewise‑linear u, OCE also yields a linear programming representation. In multi‑stage problems, time‑consistent risk measures are required to avoid dynamic inconsistency; the nested CVaR (or nested OCE) applies the risk measure stagewise, leading to a dynamic programming recursion that can be solved by Stochastic Dual Dynamic Programming (SDDP) with risk‑adjusted cuts. The risk‑adjusted cut for a stage t subproblem uses the subgradient of the risk‑adjusted cost‑to‑go function, which is a convex combination of scenario subgradients weighted by the risk‑measure’s distortion of probabilities. Computationally, the main burden is the increased number of variables (η and z_s per stage) and the need to compute risk‑adjusted expectations during the forward pass of SDDP. Recent work shows that for polyhedral risk measures, the dual of the risk‑adjusted subproblem remains a linear program, so standard Benders or SDDP machinery applies with minor modifications. Applications include energy portfolio optimization, where CVaR limits exposure to extreme price spikes, and supply chain design, where OCE captures asymmetric penalty for stockouts versus overstock.

Online Convex Optimization: Regret Minimization and Mirror Descent

Online convex optimization (OCO) models decision‑making under sequentially revealed convex loss functions f_t: X → ℝ, where X ⊂ ℝⁿ is a convex compact set. At each round t, the learner chooses x_t ∈ X, observes f_t, and incurs loss f_t(x_t). Performance is measured by regret R_T = Σt=1^T f_t(x_t) − minx∈X Σt=1^T f_t(x). The goal is to achieve sublinear regret, i.e., R_T = o(T). Gradient Descent with step size η_t = 1/√t yields O(√T) regret for Lipschitz losses over Euclidean balls. Mirror Descent generalizes this by using a Bregman divergence D_ψ(x,y) generated by a strongly convex function ψ (the mirror map). The update is xt+1 = argminx∈X { η_t ∇f_t(x_t)ᵀ x + D_ψ(x, x_t) }. For the simplex X = Δⁿ with ψ(x) = Σ_i x_i log x_i (negative entropy), Mirror Descent becomes the Exponentiated Gradient algorithm, achieving regret O(√(T log n)) for losses bounded in [0,1]. This is optimal up to constants. Adaptive step sizes such as AdaGrad (η_t = 1/√(Σs=1^t ‖∇f_s‖²)) improve practical performance and yield regret bounds that depend on the observed gradient magnitudes. For strongly convex losses, the regret can be logarithmic: O(log T). The Follow‑the‑Regularized‑Leader (FTRL) framework unifies Mirror Descent and other algorithms by maintaining a cumulative regularizer; FTRL with quadratic regularizer recovers Online Gradient Descent, while with entropy regularizer it recovers Exponentiated Gradient. In bandit settings where only the loss value f_t(x_t) is observed, gradient estimates are constructed via random perturbations (e.g., one‑point gradient estimator), leading to regret O(T3/4) for convex losses and O(√T) for strongly convex losses. Applications span online portfolio selection, ad placement, and adaptive routing in communication networks.

Robust Optimization with Decision‑Dependent Uncertainty Sets

Classical robust optimization assumes an uncertainty set U that is fixed a priori. In many applications — such as facility location where demand uncertainty depends on which facilities are opened, or vaccine allocation where disease spread depends on vaccination decisions — the uncertainty set itself depends on the first‑stage decision x. This leads to a decision‑dependent uncertainty set U(x). The robust counterpart becomes: minimize cᵀx subject to A(x) y ≤ b(x) for all y ∈ U(x), x ∈ X. When U(x) is defined by linear constraints that involve x, the robust counterpart can often be reformulated as a single‑level mixed‑integer program using duality theory, provided the inner maximization over y is a linear program for fixed x. If U(x) = { y : D y ≤ d + E x }, the worst‑case constraint is maxy: Dy ≤ d+Ex aᵀ y ≤ b. By LP duality, this is equivalent to the existence of λ ≥ 0 such that Dᵀ λ = a and (d+Ex)ᵀ λ ≤ b. The resulting constraints are bilinear in x and λ; they can be linearized by standard McCormick envelopes if x is binary, or by piecewise‑linear approximations if x is continuous. For integer x, the reformulation yields a MIP that can be solved with branch‑and‑cut. When the dependence is more complex (e.g., U(x) defined by a combinatorial structure), one can use a cutting‑plane approach that iteratively solves a master problem for x and a separation subproblem that finds the worst‑case y ∈ U(x). The separation subproblem is often a MIP itself; its dual provides a cut for the master. Recent literature identifies tractable cases: if U(x) is a polyhedron whose vertices are affine functions of x, the robust counterpart reduces to a finite set of linear constraints. Another tractable class arises when the decision‑dependent uncertainty is “rectangular” — each component of y has bounds that depend affinely on x. In that case, the robust counterpart is a linear program with additional variables. Applications include resilient network design where link capacities depend on investment decisions, and adaptive inventory control where demand distribution shifts with pricing decisions.

  • Assuming that the Lagrangian dual bound always equals the LP relaxation bound; it can be strictly tighter only when subproblems have integrality gaps.
  • Using a single Benders cut per iteration when the subproblem has multiple dual optimal solutions, ignoring Pareto‑optimal cuts that dominate.
  • Applying CVaR linearization without the auxiliary variable η, leading to an incorrect risk measure.
  • Confusing the regret bound of Online Gradient Descent (O(√T)) with that of Mirror Descent on the simplex (O(√(T log n))) and applying the wrong step‑size schedule.
  • Treating decision‑dependent uncertainty sets as fixed, thereby missing bilinear terms that require McCormick linearization or cutting‑plane separation.
  • Neglecting time‑consistency in multi‑stage risk‑averse stochastic programs, resulting in dynamically inconsistent policies.
  • Using the standard subgradient step size 1/√k for Lagrangian dual ascent when a bundle method would converge in far fewer iterations.

Self‑Concordant Barrier Functions and Polynomial‑Time Interior Point Complexity

The theoretical foundation of polynomial‑time interior point methods rests on the concept of a self‑concordant barrier. A function F defined on the interior of a convex set K is a ν‑self‑concordant barrier if it is three times continuously differentiable, tends to +∞ at the boundary of K, and satisfies the differential inequality |D³F(x)[h,h,h]| ≤ 2 (D²F(x)[h,h])3/2 for all x ∈ int(K) and all directions h. The parameter ν controls the curvature of the barrier and directly determines the iteration complexity: a path‑following method using such a barrier reaches an ε‑optimal solution in O(√ν log(1/ε)) Newton steps. For the positive orthant the logarithmic barrier −Σ log xi has ν = n; for the semidefinite cone the barrier −log det X has ν = m where X is an m×m matrix. Nesterov and Nemirovski showed that any convex cone admits a ν‑self‑concordant barrier with ν equal to the dimension of the cone, establishing that every conic program can be solved in polynomial time. In practice, the choice of barrier influences the conditioning of the Newton system and the size of the feasible neighbourhood. The logarithmic barrier for linear programming yields the classic primal‑dual predictor‑corrector algorithm with O(√n L) iterations, where L is the bit‑length of the input data. For second‑order cone programming the barrier −log(t² − ‖x‖²) has ν = 2, leading to very fast convergence. Understanding the self‑concordance parameter is essential when designing custom barriers for exotic cones such as the power cone or the exponential cone, because an ill‑chosen barrier can inflate ν and destroy the polynomial‑time guarantee.

Strong Branching, Pseudocosts, and Hybrid Branching Rules in Mixed‑Integer Programming

Branch‑and‑bound solvers spend a large fraction of their time deciding which fractional variable to branch on. Strong branching evaluates a small set of candidate variables by temporarily solving the two child LP relaxations and measuring the dual bound improvement; the variable with the largest estimated gain is selected. Because solving many LPs per node is expensive, modern solvers combine strong branching with pseudocosts. A pseudocost for variable xj records the average bound increase per unit change in xj observed during previous branchings. At the start of the search, when pseudocosts are unreliable, the solver performs strong branching on a handful of variables to initialise them. As the tree grows, pseudocosts become accurate and replace strong branching for the majority of nodes. Hybrid rules such as reliability branching use a weighted combination: if a variable’s pseudocost has been updated fewer than a threshold number of times, strong branching is invoked; otherwise the pseudocost prediction is trusted. Another enhancement is score‑based branching, where the score of a variable is the product of the estimated bound improvements on the down and up branches, often raised to a power to balance exploration and exploitation. Solvers also employ look‑ahead branching (also called strong branching with limited depth) to estimate the effect of branching on the next level of the tree. The interplay between these mechanisms determines the size of the search tree and the overall solution time. Empirical studies on MIPLIB benchmarks show that a well‑tuned hybrid rule reduces the number of nodes by 30‑50 % compared with pure pseudocost branching, while keeping the per‑node overhead modest.

Branching Rule Selection
A mixed‑integer program has 200 binary variables. At the root node, 15 variables are fractional. The solver can either (a) run strong branching on all 15 candidates, (b) run strong branching on the 5 variables with highest pseudocost variance, or (c) use reliability branching with a threshold of 3 observations. Which strategy is expected to give the best trade‑off between root‑node time and total tree size, and why?
Strategy (b) is usually preferred. Strong branching on all 15 candidates incurs 30 LP solves at the root, which dominates the root‑node time. Limiting strong branching to the 5 most uncertain variables (high pseudocost variance) yields reliable pseudocost estimates for the variables that matter most, while the remaining 10 variables rely on cheap pseudocost predictions. Reliability branching (c) would still trigger strong branching for any variable with fewer than 3 observations; at the root all variables have zero observations, so it would degenerate to full strong branching. Therefore (b) balances the cost of strong branching with the quality of the resulting pseudocosts, leading to a smaller overall tree without excessive root‑node effort.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks to compare branching strategies, focus on the cost‑accuracy trade‑off: strong branching is accurate but expensive; pseudocosts are cheap but need observations; hybrid rules switch between them based on reliability counters. Mention the typical threshold (3‑5 observations) and the score function (product of down/up gains). This shows you understand both the theory and the practical implementation used in commercial solvers.

Moment‑Based Distributionally Robust Optimization: Tractable Reformulations and Duality

Distributionally robust optimization (DRO) hedges against ambiguity in the probability distribution of uncertain parameters by optimizing over a family of distributions called an ambiguity set. Moment‑based ambiguity sets specify known moments (mean, covariance, higher‑order moments) and possibly support constraints. A common choice is the set of all distributions with given mean μ and covariance Σ supported on a polyhedron. For a linear objective c(ξ)ᵀx and linear constraints A(ξ)x ≤ b(ξ), the worst‑case expectation over this ambiguity set can be expressed as a semidefinite program (SDP) using duality of moment problems. Specifically, the worst‑case expectation of a piecewise‑linear convex function reduces to an SDP with a matrix variable representing the second‑order moment matrix. When only the first two moments are known, the dual of the moment problem yields a tractable SDP: maximize λ₀ + λᵀμ + Tr(ΛΣ) subject to a linear matrix inequality that encodes the pointwise dominance of the quadratic function λ₀ + λᵀξ + ξᵀΛξ over the original cost function. If the support is the whole space, the LMI simplifies to Λ ⪰ 0 and the quadratic majorization condition. For polyhedral support, additional linear constraints appear via the S‑lemma or Putinar’s Positivstellensatz, leading to a hierarchy of SDP relaxations that converge to the true worst‑case value. The dual perspective also provides a certificate of optimality: a feasible dual solution gives a distribution (a discrete measure with at most n+1 atoms) that attains the worst‑case bound. This dual distribution is useful for stress‑testing solutions. Moment‑based DRO is more conservative than Wasserstein DRO when the true distribution has light tails, but it avoids the need for sample data and yields polynomial‑time solvable reformulations for many practical problems such as portfolio optimization with known mean‑covariance and robust control with bounded noise moments.

Bilevel Optimization with Nonconvex Lower‑Level Problems: Value Function Reformulation and MPEC Approaches

Bilevel programs where the lower‑level problem is nonconvex (e.g., integer or nonconvex nonlinear) cannot be reformulated via KKT conditions because the lower‑level optimality conditions are neither necessary nor sufficient. A rigorous approach is the value function reformulation: replace the lower‑level problem by its optimal value function φ(x) = miny∈Y(x) f(x,y), where x are the upper‑level variables and y the lower‑level variables. The bilevel problem becomes a single‑level problem with the constraint f(x,y) ≤ φ(x) and y ∈ Y(x). Since φ(x) is typically nonconvex and nonsmooth, this reformulation is a mathematical program with equilibrium constraints (MPEC) if the lower‑level is a variational inequality, or a generalized semi‑infinite program otherwise. For integer lower‑level problems, φ(x) is piecewise constant and can be described by a finite set of linear inequalities using the integer programming value function (also known as the lower envelope). This leads to a mixed‑integer program with additional binary variables that encode which lower‑level basis is optimal. An alternative is the penalty method: add a term ρ·(f(x,y) − φ(x)) to the upper‑level objective and drive ρ → ∞. Convergence to a global optimum requires exact penalization, which holds under constraint qualifications such as the Mangasarian‑Fromovitz condition for the lower‑level feasible set. For nonconvex continuous lower‑level problems, one can use branch‑and‑bound on the lower‑level value function combined with convex relaxations (e.g., McCormick envelopes) to obtain global bounds. Recent algorithms employ a cutting‑plane scheme where the master problem optimizes over x and a lower bound on φ(x), while the subproblem solves the lower‑level problem for a fixed x to generate a supporting hyperplane (a cut) for φ(x). This is essentially a Benders‑type decomposition for bilevel problems. The approach is implemented in solvers such as BILEVEL‑BARON and has been applied to toll setting, Stackelberg games with nonconvex follower costs, and hyperparameter optimization in machine learning.

Extended Formulations and Lift‑and‑Project Methods for Combinatorial Optimization

The strength of a linear programming relaxation depends critically on the polyhedron used to model the feasible set. An extended formulation introduces auxiliary variables to obtain a higher‑dimensional polyhedron whose projection onto the original space equals the convex hull of integer solutions. The classic example is the flow formulation for the traveling salesman problem, which uses O(n²) variables and constraints and yields a much tighter relaxation than the standard subtour elimination formulation. Yannakakis proved that the TSP polytope has no polynomial‑size symmetric extended formulation, but asymmetric formulations of subexponential size exist. Lift‑and‑project methods systematically generate valid inequalities by lifting a lower‑dimensional relaxation into a higher‑dimensional space, adding cutting planes, and projecting back. The Lovász‑Schrijver (LS) operator applies a single round of lifting using the constraints xi(1−xi) = 0 for binary variables, producing the LS0 and LS+ hierarchies. The Sherali‑Adams (SA) hierarchy lifts by multiplying constraints with products of variables and their complements, yielding a sequence of LP relaxations that converge to the integer hull after n rounds. The Lasserre (or Sum‑of‑Squares) hierarchy uses semidefinite programming and converges in at most n rounds as well, but often provides much tighter bounds at low levels. In practice, the first or second round of SA or Lasserre already closes a large fraction of the integrality gap for problems such as Max‑Cut, Vertex Cover, and Stable Set. Modern solvers incorporate cutting planes from the LS+ closure (also called lift‑and‑project cuts) as a default component of their MIP engines. These cuts are generated by solving a small LP for each fractional variable, producing a rank‑1 inequality that cuts off the current fractional point. The computational overhead is modest because the LP is solved in the space of the original variables plus one auxiliary variable. Understanding the trade‑off between the size of the extended formulation and the strength of the relaxation is crucial when modelling large‑scale combinatorial problems: a compact formulation with a weak LP may be outperformed by a larger but tighter extended formulation, especially when the solver can exploit the structure of the added variables.

  • Assuming that a self‑concordant barrier with a smaller ν always yields faster practical performance; the constant factors in the Newton system dominate for moderate‑size problems.
  • Believing that strong branching alone is sufficient for large MIPs; without pseudocosts the per‑node cost becomes prohibitive.
  • Treating moment‑based DRO as equivalent to Wasserstein DRO; the former uses only moment information and can be overly conservative when higher‑order moments are ignored.
  • Applying KKT reformulation to bilevel problems with integer lower‑level variables; the resulting MPEC is invalid because KKT conditions are not necessary for integer programs.
  • Expecting that one round of lift‑and‑project always closes the integrality gap; for many problems the gap persists until higher rounds or the full hierarchy.
  • Neglecting the size‑strength trade‑off in extended formulations; a polynomial‑size extended formulation may not exist for the target polytope, forcing the use of cutting planes instead.
  • Mastering these advanced topics ensures readiness for the most challenging optimization questions.

Bilevel Mixed‑Integer Programming: Reformulations and Branch‑and‑Bound Algorithms

Bilevel mixed‑integer programs (BMIPs) arise when an upper‑level decision maker controls a subset of integer variables while a lower‑level problem, itself a mixed‑integer program, reacts optimally. Unlike continuous bilevel problems, the lower‑level value function φ(x) is piecewise constant and non‑convex, so classical KKT reformulations are invalid. A standard exact approach replaces the lower‑level problem by its value‑function epigraph: introduce a continuous variable η ≥ φ(x) and enforce η = φ(x) through a cutting‑plane scheme. For a fixed upper‑level vector x, the lower‑level MIP is solved to optimality, yielding an optimal value φ(x) and a dual proof of optimality (e.g., a feasible basis for the LP relaxation together with a branch‑and‑bound tree). From this information a supporting hyperplane (a Benders‑type cut) η ≥ φ(x̂) + πᵀ(x − x̂) is generated, where π is a subgradient derived from the LP relaxation at the leaf nodes. The master problem, a pure integer program in (x, η), is then re‑optimized. This process repeats until the master’s η equals the true lower‑level value for the incumbent x.

Because the lower‑level value function can have exponentially many pieces, the cutting‑plane loop may require many iterations. Modern solvers such as BILEVEL‑BARON and the open‑source PyBilevel combine the above scheme with strong branching on the upper‑level variables and with heuristic upper bounds obtained by fixing x and solving the lower‑level problem heuristically. An alternative exact reformulation uses a single‑level mixed‑integer program with binary expansion of the lower‑level objective: if the lower‑level objective coefficients are integral and bounded, one can introduce binary variables representing each possible objective value and enforce correctness through big‑M constraints. This yields a large but pure MIP that can be handed to a commercial solver. The trade‑off is between the number of cuts generated in the decomposition and the size of the monolithic reformulation.

Branch‑and‑bound on the upper‑level variables can be interleaved with the cut generation: at each node of the upper‑level tree, the lower‑level problem is solved for the node’s fixed x, producing a local lower bound. If the bound exceeds the current best upper bound, the node is fathomed. Pseudocosts from previous lower‑level solves guide variable selection, reducing the number of expensive lower‑level calls. Recent research shows that adding Pareto‑optimal cuts (cuts that are not dominated by any other valid cut) dramatically reduces the number of iterations. These cuts are obtained by solving an auxiliary LP that maximizes the violation of the current cut subject to the lower‑level dual feasibility region.

Applications include toll setting on transportation networks where the leader sets integer toll levels and followers choose routes, Stackelberg games with discrete follower actions, and hyper‑parameter optimization where the upper level selects integer architecture choices and the lower level trains a model. In all cases the integrality of both levels makes the problem NP‑hard even for a single follower, so algorithmic progress relies on exploiting problem‑specific structure such as network flow sub‑problems or totally unimodular lower‑level constraints.

Stochastic Programming with Endogenous Uncertainty: Decision‑Dependent Scenario Trees

In classical stochastic programming the probability distribution of uncertain parameters is exogenous. Endogenous uncertainty occurs when decisions influence the realization or the probability of future events. A typical example is oil‑field development: the decision to drill a well reveals the reservoir size, which in turn changes the distribution of future production. Modeling this requires a scenario tree whose branching structure depends on first‑stage decisions. One formulation introduces binary variables zₛ that indicate whether scenario s becomes active; the probability of scenario s is then a function pₛ(z) of earlier decisions. The non‑anticipativity constraints are written only for scenarios that share the same history up to the current stage, which can be enforced by linking the decision variables of scenarios with identical prefixes through equality constraints that are activated by the corresponding z variables.

The resulting model is a large mixed‑integer program with a combinatorial number of scenarios. Decomposition methods are essential. A popular approach extends the Progressive Hedging algorithm: each scenario sub‑problem is solved with a penalty term that forces the first‑stage decisions to agree across scenarios that are currently active. The penalty parameter is updated iteratively, and the binary activation variables are relaxed to continuous variables in [0,1] during the sub‑problem solves, then rounded in a master MIP that enforces consistency of the scenario tree. Another line of work uses Lagrangian decomposition on the non‑anticipativity constraints, yielding a dual problem that can be solved by a sub‑gradient method; the sub‑gradient evaluation requires solving independent scenario sub‑problems, which is embarrassingly parallel.

Scenario reduction techniques must respect the decision‑dependent structure. Standard distance‑based reduction (e.g., Kantorovich distance) can be applied after fixing a candidate first‑stage decision, but the reduced tree must be re‑evaluated when the decision changes. Adaptive sampling methods generate scenarios on‑the‑fly: at each iteration a set of first‑stage decisions is proposed, the corresponding scenario trees are constructed, and the lower bound is updated. Convergence guarantees rely on the law of large numbers for the sampled trees and on the continuity of the value function with respect to the decision‑dependent probabilities.

Key applications include capacity expansion under learning‑by‑doing, clinical trial design where enrollment decisions affect patient‑population statistics, and cyber‑security investment where monitoring choices alter attack‑likelihood distributions. The main computational bottleneck remains the exponential growth of the scenario tree with the number of endogenous decision points, motivating research on compact extended formulations that capture the decision‑dependent branching without enumerating all scenarios.

Modeling Endogenous Uncertainty in a Two‑Stage Capacity Expansion Problem
A power company must decide in stage 1 how many MW of wind capacity (integer variable w) to build. In stage 2 the actual wind resource is revealed. The probability of a high‑wind scenario (H) is 0.6 if w ≥ 100 MW, otherwise 0.3. The low‑wind scenario (L) has the complementary probability. The second‑stage cost is 10 × shortfall in MW, where shortfall = max(0, demand − w − wind). Demand is 150 MW. Wind output is 80 MW in H and 20 MW in L. Formulate the two‑stage stochastic program with endogenous probabilities using binary activation variables.
Introduce binary variables z_H, z_L ∈ {0,1} indicating whether scenario H or L is active. The first‑stage decision w ∈ ℤ₊. Probability of H: p_H = 0.6·z_H + 0.3·(1−z_H) = 0.3 + 0.3·z_H. Since exactly one scenario occurs, enforce z_H + z_L = 1. Link activation to w: z_H = 1 ⇔ w ≥ 100. This can be modeled with big‑M: w − 100 ≥ −M·(1−z_H) and w − 99 ≤ M·z_H. Second‑stage variables: shortfall_H, shortfall_L ≥ 0. Constraints: shortfall_H ≥ 150 − w − 80, shortfall_L ≥ 150 − w − 20. Objective: minimize 0·w + p_H·10·shortfall_H + (1−p_H)·10·shortfall_L. Substitute p_H expression. The resulting model is a mixed‑integer linear program with binary z_H, z_L and integer w. Solving yields optimal w = 100 MW, activating the high‑probability high‑wind scenario and giving expected cost 10·(0.6·0 + 0.4·50) = 200.
Exam‑Day Strategy for Endogenous Uncertainty
When a question asks to formulate a stochastic program with decision‑dependent probabilities, first identify the decision that influences the distribution. Introduce binary activation variables for each scenario and write the probability as an affine function of those binaries. Then enforce the logical link between the first‑stage decision and the activation variables using big‑M constraints. Remember to keep non‑anticipativity only among scenarios that share the same history; this is automatically satisfied if each scenario has its own second‑stage variables and the activation variables partition the scenario set. In a time‑pressured exam, sketch the scenario tree, write the probability expressions, and then the MILP – do not attempt to solve it unless explicitly required.

Data‑Driven Distributionally Robust Optimization: Ambiguity Sets from Empirical Distributions

Distributionally robust optimization (DRO) hedges against distributional ambiguity by optimizing over a family ℙ of probability distributions (the ambiguity set) rather than a single nominal distribution. Data‑driven DRO constructs ℙ from a finite sample {ξ¹,…,ξᴺ}. The most common ambiguity sets are: (i) the Wasserstein ball ℙ_W = {ℚ : W_p(ℚ, ℙ̂_N) ≤ ε}, where ℙ̂_N is the empirical distribution and W_p is the p‑Wasserstein distance; (ii) the moment‑based set ℙ_M = {ℚ : 𝔼_ℚ[φ(ξ)] ∈ [μ̂ − δ, μ̂ + δ]} for a vector of feature functions φ; (iii) the φ‑divergence ball ℙ_φ = {ℚ : D_φ(ℚ‖ℙ̂_N) ≤ η}. Each choice yields a different tractable reformulation. For the Wasserstein ball with p = 1 and a convex piecewise‑linear loss, the worst‑case expectation can be expressed as a linear program with N additional variables and constraints, leading to a finite convex program that scales linearly with the sample size. Strong duality of the Wasserstein DRO problem holds under mild conditions (e.g., the loss function is upper‑semicontinuous and the support is compact), and the optimal value converges to the true robust optimum at rate O(N−1/d) where d is the dimension of ξ.

Moment‑based ambiguity sets lead to semidefinite programs when the feature functions are polynomials and the support is described by polynomial inequalities (via the Lasserre hierarchy). The size of the SDP grows combinatorially with the degree of the moment relaxation, so in practice only first‑ or second‑order moments are used, yielding a tractable SDP with a single matrix variable. φ‑divergence balls (e.g., KL‑divergence, χ²‑divergence) admit convex conic reformulations: the worst‑case expectation becomes a convex optimization problem with an exponential cone (for KL) or a rotated quadratic cone (for χ²). Modern conic solvers (MOSEK, ECOS) handle these cones efficiently.

Statistical guarantees are central. For Wasserstein DRO, if ε_N = C·(log(1/β)/N)1/d then with probability at least 1−β the true distribution lies in the ball, and the DRO solution is feasible for the true problem. For moment‑based sets, concentration inequalities (e.g., Bernstein) give confidence intervals for the moments, which translate into δ. The choice of ambiguity set reflects the decision maker’s trust in the data: Wasserstein captures geometry of the sample space, moment sets encode known structural information (e.g., known mean and covariance), and φ‑divergence sets are natural when the nominal model is a parametric family estimated by maximum likelihood.

Recent extensions combine multiple ambiguity sets (intersection of Wasserstein and moment balls) to exploit both geometric and moment information. Algorithmic approaches include cutting‑plane methods that separate over the ambiguity set, and stochastic gradient descent on the dual formulation when the loss is smooth. Applications span portfolio optimization with heavy‑tailed returns, robust control of queueing systems with unknown arrival distributions, and fair machine learning where the ambiguity set enforces demographic parity constraints in expectation.

Primal‑Dual Hybrid Gradient Methods for Large‑Scale Saddle‑Point Problems

The primal‑dual hybrid gradient (PDHG) method, also known as the Chambolle‑Pock algorithm, solves convex‑concave saddle‑point problems of the form min_x max_y ⟨Kx, y⟩ + f(x) − g*(y), where f and g* are proper closed convex functions (possibly non‑smooth) and K is a linear operator. The algorithm maintains primal iterate x^k and dual iterate y^k and updates them by
xk+1 = proxτ f(x^k − τ K^* y^k)
yk+1 = proxσ g*(y^k + σ K (2 xk+1 − x^k))
where τ, σ > 0 are step sizes satisfying τσ‖K‖² < 1, and prox denotes the proximal operator. The over‑relaxation factor θ = 1 yields the standard scheme; θ ∈ [0,1] can accelerate convergence for strongly convex f or g*. The method enjoys an O(1/k) ergodic convergence rate in the primal‑dual gap, and O(1/k²) when both f and g* are strongly convex.

A key practical advantage is that the proximal operators of f and g* are often available in closed form. For example, if f(x) = ‖x‖₁ (ℓ₁ regularization) then proxτ f is soft‑thresholding; if g*(y) = ι‖y‖_∞ ≤ λ (indicator of an ℓ_∞ ball) then proxσ g* is projection onto the ℓ_∞ ball. The linear operator K can represent a discrete gradient (total variation denoising), a Radon transform (tomography), or a constraint matrix in linear programming. In the latter case, PDHG becomes a first‑order primal‑dual method for LPs that avoids solving linear systems, making it attractive for huge‑scale problems where interior‑point methods are memory‑bound.

Convergence diagnostics rely on the primal‑dual gap G^k = f(x^k) + g*(y^k) + ⟨Kx^k, y^k⟩. Since the exact gap is often unavailable, a computable surrogate uses the Bregman divergence of the iterates. Adaptive step‑size rules (e.g., the diagonal preconditioning τ_i = 1/√(∑_j Kij²), σ_j = 1/√(∑_i Kij²)) improve practical speed by balancing the scaling of primal and dual variables. The algorithm is naturally parallelizable: the proximal updates for separable f and g* decompose across coordinates, and the matrix‑vector products Kx and K^*y can be distributed.

Applications include large‑scale image reconstruction (total variation, ℓ₁‑wavelet), optimal transport with entropic regularization (where K is the cost matrix and g* is the negative entropy), and distributed optimization over networks (each node holds a block of x, consensus enforced by dual variables). Recent variants incorporate momentum (accelerated PDHG) and stochastic estimates of K for problems where K is a sum of many components (e.g., finite‑sum saddle‑point problems). The method’s simplicity, low per‑iteration cost, and strong theoretical guarantees make it a workhorse for modern large‑scale convex optimization.

Quantum‑Inspired Heuristics for Combinatorial Optimization

Quantum‑inspired algorithms mimic quantum mechanical phenomena — superposition, tunneling, and entanglement — on classical hardware to explore combinatorial search spaces. The most prominent examples are simulated quantum annealing (SQA), the quantum approximate optimization algorithm (QAOA) simulated via tensor‑network contraction, and population‑based methods that use quantum‑style interference (e.g., quantum‑inspired genetic algorithms). SQA replaces the quantum Hamiltonian by a path‑integral representation with P Trotter slices, yielding a classical Ising model on a (P+1)‑dimensional lattice. Monte‑Carlo updates on this extended system replicate quantum tunneling through a thermal‑like transition across slices. The computational cost scales linearly with P, and choosing P ≈ β√N (β inverse temperature, N problem size) often captures the essential quantum speedup for problems with tall, thin barriers.

QAOA prepares a parameterized quantum state by alternating problem‑Hamiltonian and mixer‑Hamiltonian unitaries. On a classical computer the expectation value of the cost function can be approximated by contracting a tensor network whose bond dimension grows with the circuit depth. For low‑depth QAOA (p ≤ 3) on sparse graphs (e.g., Max‑Cut on degree‑3 graphs) the contraction is feasible and yields high‑quality solutions. The variational parameters are optimized by gradient‑free methods (Nelder‑Mead, Bayesian optimization) because the landscape is non‑convex but low‑dimensional.

Quantum‑inspired genetic algorithms maintain a population of probability amplitudes (complex numbers) instead of binary strings. Evolutionary operators are defined as unitary rotations in the amplitude space, and measurement collapses amplitudes to classical candidates for evaluation. This approach has shown empirical success on knapsack, graph coloring, and vehicle routing instances up to several thousand variables, often outperforming standard genetic algorithms when the fitness landscape contains many local optima separated by high barriers.

Despite promising benchmarks, rigorous complexity‑theoretic guarantees are scarce. Most results are empirical or rely on heuristic arguments about tunneling rates. Moreover, the overhead of simulating quantum dynamics (extra Trotter slices, tensor‑network bond dimensions) can outweigh benefits for problems where classical heuristics already perform well. Current research focuses on hybrid schemes: use a quantum‑inspired sampler to generate high‑quality initial solutions for a branch‑and‑bound MIP solver, or embed a quantum‑inspired local search inside a large‑neighborhood search framework. As quantum hardware matures, these classical simulators serve as both algorithmic testbeds and fallback solvers for problem sizes beyond near‑term quantum devices.

  • Assuming that a bilevel mixed‑integer program can be solved by simply applying KKT conditions to the lower level; the resulting MPEC is invalid because KKT conditions are not necessary for integer programs.
  • Treating endogenous uncertainty as exogenous and using a fixed scenario tree; this ignores the decision‑dependent branching and leads to infeasible or suboptimal policies.
  • Choosing a Wasserstein radius ε without statistical calibration; an overly small ε yields no robustness, while an overly large ε makes the problem overly conservative and numerically ill‑conditioned.
  • Using a constant step size τσ‖K‖² = 1 in PDHG; the strict inequality τσ‖K‖² < 1 is required for convergence, and violating it can cause divergence.
  • Expecting quantum‑inspired heuristics to provably outperform classical metaheuristics on all instances; performance is highly problem‑dependent and often comparable to well‑tuned simulated annealing.
  • Neglecting the need for non‑anticipativity constraints only among scenarios sharing the same history in endogenous stochastic programs; omitting them creates a model that allows the decision maker to anticipate future observations.
  • Believing that a single round of lift‑and‑project cuts always closes the integrality gap for bilevel MIPs; the gap may persist and require higher rounds or a full hierarchy.

The advanced topics above extend the core operations research toolkit and are essential for tackling the most demanding optimization problems in contemporary practice.

Metaheuristic Search Strategies for Large‑Scale Combinatorial Optimization

Metaheuristics provide a framework for exploring vast discrete search spaces when exact methods become computationally prohibitive. The most widely studied families include simulated annealing (SA), tabu search (TS), genetic algorithms (GA), ant colony optimization (ACO), and variable neighborhood search (VNS). Each method balances intensification (exploiting promising regions) and diversification (escaping local optima) through distinct mechanisms.

Simulated Annealing

SA mimics the physical annealing process by accepting worsening moves with a probability that decays according to a temperature schedule T(k). A classic logarithmic schedule T(k) = T₀ / log(1+k) guarantees asymptotic convergence to a global optimum for finite state spaces, but practical implementations use geometric cooling T(k+1) = α·T(k) with α ∈ [0.85,0.99] for faster runtimes. Neighborhood structures (e.g., 2‑opt for TSP, swap for knapsack) critically affect performance; adaptive neighborhood selection can improve robustness.

Tabu Search

TS maintains a short‑term memory (tabu list) of recently visited solutions or move attributes to prevent cycling. Aspiration criteria override tabu status when a move yields a solution better than the best known. Strategic oscillation between feasible and infeasible regions, guided by penalty functions, enables TS to handle constrained problems such as vehicle routing with time windows. Long‑term memory (frequency‑based diversification) and path‑relinking (combining elite solutions) further enhance solution quality.

Genetic Algorithms

GA operates on a population of chromosomes encoded as binary strings, permutations, or real vectors. Selection (tournament, roulette‑wheel), crossover (order‑based for permutations, uniform for binary), and mutation (swap, inversion) drive evolution. Hybrid GA (memetic algorithms) embed a local search (e.g., 2‑opt) after crossover to accelerate convergence. Parameter tuning (population size 100‑500, crossover probability 0.7‑0.9, mutation probability 0.01‑0.1) is problem‑dependent; adaptive schemes adjust rates based on diversity metrics.

Ant Colony Optimization

ACO constructs solutions by simulating pheromone deposition on graph edges. The transition probability for an ant moving from node i to j is proportional to τᵢⱼ^α · ηᵢⱼ^β, where τ is pheromone level, η is heuristic desirability (e.g., inverse distance), and α,β control exploitation vs. exploration. Pheromone update τᵢⱼ ← (1‑ρ)·τᵢⱼ + Δτᵢⱼ with evaporation rate ρ ∈ [0.1,0.5] prevents premature convergence. Variants such as MAX‑MIN Ant System bound τ to avoid stagnation.

Variable Neighborhood Search

VNS systematically changes neighborhoods during a local search: shaking (random move in a larger neighborhood) followed by descent in the current neighborhood. The neighborhood sequence (e.g., 1‑opt, 2‑opt, 3‑opt for TSP) is predefined; if improvement occurs, the search restarts from the first neighborhood. VNS is simple to implement and often matches or exceeds more complex metaheuristics on benchmark instances.

Empirical studies on benchmark libraries (TSPLIB, CVRPLIB, OR‑Library) show that well‑tuned hybrid metaheuristics (e.g., GA+TS, ACO+VNS) consistently achieve gaps < 1 % from best known solutions for instances up to several thousand variables, while pure exact methods (branch‑and‑cut) often stall beyond a few hundred variables.

Conic Optimization Beyond Second‑Order Cones: Power Cones and Exponential Cones

Modern conic solvers (MOSEK, ECOS, SCS) support a hierarchy of cones that extends the familiar second‑order cone (SOC). Two pivotal additions are the three‑dimensional power cone and the three‑dimensional exponential cone, which enable exact modeling of a broad class of non‑linear convex constraints without resorting to approximations.

Power Cone

The power cone 𝒦_pow(α) = { (x,y,z) ∈ ℝ³ | x^α y1‑α ≥ |z|, x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0 } with parameter α ∈ (0,1) generalizes the geometric mean inequality. For α = ½ it reduces to the rotated quadratic cone. Any constraint of the form ∏i=1^k t_iw_i ≥ s with weights w_i > 0, Σ w_i = 1 can be represented by a chain of power cones, yielding a disciplined convex programming (DCP) formulation for geometric programs (GP). This representation is exact and preserves polynomial‑time interior‑point complexity.

Exponential Cone

The exponential cone 𝒦_exp = { (x,y,z) ∈ ℝ³ | y·exp(x/y) ≤ z, y > 0 } ∪ { (x,0,z) | x ≤ 0, z ≥ 0 } captures the perspective of the exponential function. It allows direct modeling of entropy terms (x·log(x/y)), log‑sum‑exp constraints, and relative entropy minimization. For instance, the constraint Σ_i exp(a_i^T u + b_i) ≤ t is equivalent to introducing auxiliary variables and exponential cone constraints, avoiding the need for successive linear approximations.

Modeling Applications

  • Geometric programming: posynomial minimization → power‑cone formulation.
  • Maximum likelihood estimation for log‑normal models: exponential‑cone constraints.
  • Portfolio optimization with transaction costs modeled via perspective functions: power cones.
  • Quantum entropy optimization in quantum information theory: exponential cones.

Both cones are self‑dual (up to a linear transformation), which simplifies the derivation of dual problems and enables primal‑dual interior‑point methods to maintain the same iteration complexity O(√ν log(1/ε)) where ν is the barrier parameter (ν = 3 for each three‑dimensional cone). Implementations in MOSEK 10+ and CVXPY 1.3+ expose these cones through native atom libraries, allowing modelers to write GP or entropy objectives directly.

Worked Example: Power‑Cone Reformulation of a Geometric Program
Reformulate the posynomial minimization problem
minimize x₁⁻⁰·⁵ x₂⁰·³ + 2 x₁⁰·² x₂⁻⁰·⁴
subject to x₁ > 0, x₂ > 0
as a conic program using power cones.
Step 1. Introduce epigraph variables t₁, t₂ for each term and a variable s for the objective: minimize s subject to t₁ + t₂ ≤ s.
Step 2. Each term is a monomial. For the first term x₁⁻⁰·⁵ x₂⁰·³ ≤ t₁, rewrite as (x₁⁻¹)⁰·⁵ (x₂)⁰·³ ≤ t₁. Set u₁ = x₁⁻¹, u₂ = x₂. The inequality u₁⁰·⁵ u₂⁰·³ ≤ t₁ is a power‑cone constraint with α = 0.5/(0.5+0.3) = 5/8. Introduce auxiliary variable v₁ and enforce (u₁, u₂, v₁) ∈ 𝒦_pow(5/8) and v₁ ≤ t₁.
Step 3. Similarly, second term 2 x₁⁰·² x₂⁻⁰·⁴ ≤ t₂ → (x₁, x₂⁻¹, v₂) ∈ 𝒦_pow(0.2/(0.2+0.4)=1/3) with v₂ ≤ t₂/2.
Step 4. The reciprocal variables are modeled via rotated quadratic cones: (x₁, 1, u₁) ∈ 𝒦_rquad, (x₂, 1, u₂) ∈ 𝒦_rquad, etc.
Result. The entire problem is a conic program with only power cones, rotated quadratic cones, and linear constraints, solvable in polynomial time by any modern interior‑point conic solver.
Exam‑Day Strategy for Conic Modeling
When a question asks to “express as a conic program”, first identify any geometric‑program or entropy terms. Replace each posynomial inequality with a chain of power‑cone constraints (remember the weight α = w_i / Σ w_j). Replace log‑sum‑exp or relative entropy terms with exponential‑cone constraints. Keep a cheat‑sheet of the standard cone definitions (SOC, rotated SOC, power, exponential) and their barrier parameters; examiners often test whether you know that both power and exponential cones have ν = 3.

Risk‑Averse Stochastic Programming with Spectral and Distortion Risk Measures

Classical stochastic programming minimizes expected cost, which is risk‑neutral. Risk‑averse formulations replace the expectation with a coherent risk measure ρ(·) that satisfies monotonicity, translation invariance, positive homogeneity, and subadditivity. Two important families beyond Conditional Value‑at‑Risk (CVaR) are spectral risk measures and distortion risk measures.

Spectral Risk Measures

A spectral risk measure is defined by a non‑decreasing, non‑negative weighting function φ: [0,1] → ℝ₊ with ∫₀¹ φ(p) dp = 1. For a random loss L with quantile function q_L(p) = inf{ℓ | P(L ≤ ℓ) ≥ p}, the spectral measure is ρ_φ(L) = ∫₀¹ φ(p) q_L(p) dp. CVaR at level β corresponds to φ(p) = (1/(1‑β))·1p>β. By choosing φ that puts more weight on high quantiles (e.g., φ(p) = c·pk), the decision maker can express increasing aversion to tail losses. Spectral measures are law‑invariant and admit a linear programming representation when the loss distribution is discrete: introduce variables η_p for each scenario and enforce η_p ≥ loss_s for all scenarios s with probability weight p_s, then minimize Σ φ(p) η_p.

Distortion Risk Measures

A distortion risk measure uses a non‑decreasing distortion function g: [0,1] → [0,1] with g(0)=0, g(1)=1. The risk of L is ρ_g(L) = ∫₀^∞ g(P(L > t)) dt − ∫‑∞0 [1‑g(P(L ≤ t))] dt. For discrete distributions this reduces to ρ_g(L) = Σi=1^n (g(Σj=1^i p(j)) − g(Σj=1i‑1 p(j)))·ℓ(i), where ℓ(i) are ordered losses. The Wang transform g(p) = Φ(Φ⁻¹(p) + θ) (Φ standard normal CDF) yields a popular one‑parameter family that interpolates between expectation (θ=0) and essential supremum (θ→∞). Distortion measures are also law‑invariant and can be linearized via auxiliary variables and ordering constraints.

Optimization with Risk Measures

In a two‑stage stochastic program, the first‑stage decision x minimizes c^T x + ρ(Q(x,ξ)), where Q(x,ξ) is the second‑stage value function. When ρ is spectral or distortion, the problem can be reformulated as a large‑scale linear program (for discrete ξ) or a conic program (if ρ is represented via epigraph of a convex piecewise‑linear function). Decomposition methods (Benders, L‑shaped) extend naturally: the risk measure appears in the master objective, and subgradients are computed from the dual of the risk‑measure LP. Sample Average Approximation (SAA) provides statistical guarantees; the number of scenarios needed grows with the Lipschitz constant of φ or g.

Practical Considerations

  • Eliciting φ or g from stakeholders: use pairwise comparisons of lotteries to fit a parametric family.
  • Computational burden: spectral measures require O(N) extra variables per scenario; distortion measures need ordering constraints that can be modeled with binary variables for exact representation, or approximated by piecewise‑linear g.
  • Regulatory relevance: Solvency II and Basel III frameworks accept spectral/distortion measures for capital allocation.

Scenario Tree Construction via Clustering and Moment Matching

Multi‑stage stochastic programs require a scenario tree that approximates the underlying stochastic process while preserving non‑anticipativity. Two complementary approaches dominate practice: (i) forward tree construction by clustering sampled paths, and (ii) moment‑matching tree generation that enforces prescribed statistical properties at each stage.

Path‑Based Clustering

Generate a large Monte‑Carlo sample of full‑horizon paths {ξ^s}s=1^S. At stage t, cluster the prefixes (ξ₁^s,…,ξ_t^s) using k‑means, hierarchical agglomerative clustering, or a Wasserstein‑barycenter approach. Each cluster becomes a node; the conditional probability of a child node is the fraction of paths in the parent cluster that fall into the child cluster. The number of branches per node (branching factor) controls tree size; adaptive branching (more branches where variance is high) improves accuracy. The Kantorovich (Wasserstein) distance between the original process distribution and the tree distribution provides an error bound: if the clustering radius at stage t is ε_t, the total tree error ≤ Σ_t ε_t.

Moment‑Matching Trees

Instead of sampling, specify target moments (mean, covariance, skewness, kurtosis) for each stage conditional on the history. A moment‑matching tree of degree d at each node is constructed by solving a small nonlinear system that places d child nodes with probabilities p_i and values ξ_i such that Σ_i p_i ξ_i^k = μ_k for k=1,…,2d‑1. For multivariate ξ, match cross‑moments up to order two (covariance) using a quadrature rule (e.g., Gauss‑Hermite for Gaussian marginals). The resulting tree exactly reproduces the prescribed moments, which is valuable when the stochastic process is defined by moment conditions (e.g., factor models in finance).

Hybrid Schemes and Reduction

In practice, a hybrid approach is common: generate a dense tree by clustering, then apply scenario reduction (fast forward selection) to prune low‑probability nodes while bounding the increase in the Wasserstein distance. The reduction algorithm iteratively removes the node whose deletion causes the smallest increase in the distance, recomputing probabilities of remaining nodes. Theoretical guarantees state that removing a node with probability p increases the distance by at most p·diameter of its cluster.

Endogenous Uncertainty

When decisions affect the information structure (endogenous uncertainty), the tree must branch on decision‑dependent events. A common technique is to introduce binary “observation” variables that activate child nodes only when certain first‑stage decisions are taken. The resulting tree is no longer a fixed structure but a decision‑dependent scenario DAG; non‑anticipativity constraints are enforced only among scenarios sharing the same history up to the decision point.

Lagrangian Relaxation for Capacitated Facility Location and Network Design

The capacitated facility location problem (CFLP) and its network design extensions are NP‑hard mixed‑integer programs where Lagrangian relaxation provides strong dual bounds and effective heuristics. The standard formulation minimizes Σ_i f_i y_i + Σi,j cij xij subject to Σ_i xij = d_j (demand satisfaction), Σ_j d_j xij ≤ u_i y_i (capacity), xij ≤ y_i (linking), y_i ∈ {0,1}, xij ≥ 0.

Relaxing Capacity Constraints

Dualize the capacity constraints Σ_j d_j xij ≤ u_i y_i with multipliers λ_i ≥ 0. The Lagrangian subproblem separates by facility i: for each i, minimize f_i y_i + Σ_j (cij + λ_i d_j) xij − λ_i u_i y_i subject to Σ_i xij = d_j, xij ≤ y_i, y_i ∈ {0,1}, xij ≥ 0. Because the demand constraints couple facilities, a second relaxation (or a column‑generation pricing step) is often applied. Alternatively, relax the demand constraints with multipliers π_j, yielding independent facility subproblems that are simple knapsack‑type problems solvable in O(n log n) by greedy sorting of reduced costs.

Subgradient Optimization

The dual function θ(λ,π) is concave but non‑differentiable.
