The subgradient method updates multipliers: λ_ik+1 = max{0, λ_i^k + α_k (Σ_j d_j xij^k − u_i y_i^k)} with step size α_k = β_k (UB − θ^k) / ‖g^k‖², where UB is the best known primal feasible objective, β_k ∈ (0,2) (often 1.5 initially, decreasing). Convergence is guaranteed to the optimal dual value; the dual bound typically closes 90‑98 % of the integrality gap for CFLP instances up to 500 facilities.

Primal Recovery Heuristics

From a near‑optimal dual solution, construct a feasible primal solution: (1) fix y_i = 1 for facilities with y_i^k = 1 in the last subproblem; (2) solve the resulting transportation problem (LP) to obtain xij; (3) if capacity violated, apply a greedy reassignment of demand to nearby open facilities with residual capacity; (4) optionally run a local search (swap open/closed facilities) to improve the objective. This heuristic often yields solutions within 1‑3 % of the true optimum.

Network Design Extension

For capacitated network design (binary arc‑selection variables z_a, flow variables f_a), relax flow‑capacity coupling constraints f_a ≤ u_a z_a. The subproblem becomes a minimum‑cost flow with fixed arc costs plus Lagrangian terms, solvable by a single shortest‑path or min‑cost‑flow computation per iteration. The dual bound is strong enough to drive branch‑and‑bound with very few nodes on benchmark instances (e.g., ND‑lib).

  • Assuming that a metaheuristic’s parameter settings (e.g., SA cooling rate) can be transferred unchanged across problem classes; performance is highly sensitive to neighborhood structure and instance scale.
  • Treating power‑cone and exponential‑cone constraints as interchangeable; they model fundamentally different function classes (geometric mean vs. exponential perspective) and have distinct barrier parameters.
  • Using CVaR as the sole risk measure without checking whether the decision maker’s preferences satisfy the spectral weighting function’s monotonicity; a misspecified φ can lead to non‑coherent risk assessments.
  • Building a scenario tree by naive Monte‑Carlo sampling without clustering or moment matching; the resulting tree often violates non‑anticipativity or yields a poor approximation of the true process distribution.
  • Applying Lagrangian relaxation to the wrong set of constraints (e.g., relaxing linking constraints instead of capacities) which destroys the separability needed for efficient subproblem solution.
  • Neglecting to update the step‑size parameter β_k in subgradient optimization; a constant β leads to oscillations and failure to converge to the dual optimum.
  • Believing that a single round of scenario reduction preserves the Wasserstein distance bound; iterative reduction with recomputed distances is required for theoretical guarantees.

The advanced techniques described above expand the operations research toolkit for tackling large‑scale, risk‑aware, and combinatorially complex optimization problems encountered in modern practice.

L‑Shaped Method and Regularized Decomposition for Two‑Stage Stochastic Linear Programs

The L‑shaped method, introduced by Van Slyke and Wets in 1969, solves two‑stage stochastic linear programs with recourse by decomposing the problem into a master program and a set of scenario subproblems. The first‑stage decisions x appear in the master, while each scenario ω yields a second‑stage linear program min { qωᵀyω : Wωyω = hω − Tωx, yω ≥ 0 }. The dual of each subproblem provides an optimality cut θ ≥ πωᵀ(hω − Tωx) that is added to the master. When a subproblem is infeasible, a feasibility cut πωᵀ(hω − Tωx) ≤ 0 is generated from an extreme ray of the dual. The algorithm iterates until the master objective equals the expected recourse cost within tolerance.

Regularized decomposition (Ruszczynski, 1986) stabilizes the master by adding a quadratic proximal term ½ρ‖x − x̄‖² to the master objective, where x̄ is the current iterate and ρ > 0 a penalty parameter. This yields a sequence of strictly convex quadratic programs that can be solved efficiently with active‑set or interior‑point methods. The regularization prevents oscillatory behavior when cuts are nearly parallel and improves convergence on problems with many scenarios. A practical variant, the level‑method, replaces the quadratic term by a level set constraint cᵀx + θ ≤ L where L is updated adaptively; this avoids the need to tune ρ and often reduces the number of master solves.

Implementation details matter: (1) cut aggregation — grouping scenarios into bundles reduces master size but weakens cuts; (2) cut selection — removing inactive cuts keeps the master small; (3) warm‑starting subproblem solves with the previous dual basis accelerates each iteration. For large‑scale instances (e.g., 10⁴ scenarios, 10³ first‑stage variables) the regularized L‑shaped method with cut aggregation typically solves in a few hundred iterations, whereas the classical method may stall due to degeneracy.

Integer L‑Shaped Method for Stochastic Programs with Integer Recourse

When second‑stage variables are required to be integer, the recourse function becomes non‑convex and the classical L‑shaped cuts are no longer valid. The integer L‑shaped method (Laporte & Louveaux, 1993) circumvent this by generating cuts from the LP relaxation of each scenario subproblem and then strengthening them with integer‑specific cuts. For a given first‑stage solution x̄, each scenario subproblem is solved as a mixed‑integer program. If the LP relaxation is infeasible, a feasibility cut from the dual ray is added. If the LP relaxation is feasible but the integer program is infeasible, a combinatorial feasibility cut (e.g., a no‑good cut) is derived from the integer infeasibility proof. When both LP and IP are feasible, an optimality cut is constructed using the dual solution of the LP relaxation together with a lifting procedure that accounts for the integrality gap.

A key ingredient is the value function Q(x) = Σω pω Qω(x) where Qω is the optimal integer recourse cost. Because Q is piecewise constant and lower semicontinuous, the master problem becomes a mixed‑integer program with a convex envelope approximated by cuts. The algorithm alternates between solving the master MIP and the scenario MIPs. Convergence is finite because the master feasible region is a polyhedron with a finite number of integer points. In practice, the method is enhanced by (i) solving scenario subproblems only when the master solution changes significantly, (ii) using strong branching on first‑stage variables to generate tighter cuts early, and (iii) employing a branch‑and‑cut framework where integer L‑shaped cuts are added as lazy constraints during the branch‑and‑bound tree exploration.

Applications include stochastic facility location, vehicle routing with uncertain demand, and capacity expansion planning. Benchmark studies show that the integer L‑shaped method with cut aggregation and warm‑starts solves instances with up to 200 scenarios and 500 integer second‑stage variables within a few hours, whereas a monolithic MIP formulation often fails to close the root gap.

Worked Example: Integer L‑Shaped Cut Generation
Consider a two‑stage stochastic program with one first‑stage binary variable x ∈ {0,1} and two equally likely scenarios. In scenario 1 the second‑stage problem is min y₁ s.t. 2y₁ ≥ 3 − 4x, y₁ ∈ ℤ₊. In scenario 2 it is min y₂ s.t. y₂ ≥ 2 − x, y₂ ∈ ℤ₊. For the master solution x = 0, compute the optimality cut that should be added to the master.
For x = 0, scenario 1 LP relaxation: min y₁ s.t. 2y₁ ≥ 3, y₁ ≥ 0 → y₁ = 1.5, dual π₁ = 0.5. Integer optimum: y₁ = 2 (since y₁ integer). Scenario 2 LP: min y₂ s.t. y₂ ≥ 2, y₂ ≥ 0 → y₂ = 2, dual π₂ = 1. Integer optimum: y₂ = 2. Expected recourse cost = 0.5·2 + 0.5·2 = 2. The LP‑based optimality cut uses duals: θ ≥ 0.5·(3 − 4x)·0.5 + 1·(2 − x)·1 = 0.75 − x + 2 − x = 2.75 − 2x. However, because the integer optimum in scenario 1 is 2 while the LP value is 1.5, the cut underestimates the true cost. A lifted integer cut adds the integrality gap: θ ≥ 2.75 − 2x + 0.5·0.5 = 3 − 2x. For x = 0 this gives θ ≥ 3, matching the true expected cost 2? Wait, recalc: true expected cost = 0.5·2 + 0.5·2 = 2. The lifted cut should be exact at x = 0. The correct integer L‑shaped cut is θ ≥ 2 − 2x? Let's derive properly: The value function Q(x) = 0.5·ceil((3−4x)/2) + 0.5·ceil(2−x). For x=0: ceil(1.5)=2, ceil(2)=2 → Q=2. For x=1: ceil(-0.5)=0, ceil(1)=1 → Q=0.5. The convex envelope of these two points yields cut θ ≥ 2 − 1.5x. The integer L‑shaped algorithm would generate this cut after solving both integer subproblems and computing the subgradient of the convex envelope. The final cut added is θ ≥ 2 − 1.5x.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the cut generated by the integer L‑shaped method, first solve each scenario as an integer program for the given first‑stage solution. Then compute the convex envelope of the resulting value‑function points; the supporting hyperplane at the current iterate is the required cut. Remember that LP‑based duals alone are insufficient — always account for the integrality gap via lifting or envelope construction.

Distributionally Robust Optimization with Moment‑Based Ambiguity Sets and SDP Reformulations

Distributionally robust optimization (DRO) hedges against ambiguity in the probability distribution of uncertain parameters by optimizing over a family of distributions. Moment‑based ambiguity sets specify the first‑ and second‑order moments: 𝒫 = { ℙ : 𝔼ℙ[ξ] = μ, 𝔼ℙ[(ξ−μ)(ξ−μ)ᵀ] ⪯ Σ, ℙ supported on Ξ }. When the loss function is piecewise linear or quadratic in ξ, the worst‑case expectation supℙ∈𝒫 𝔼ℙ[f(x,ξ)] can be reformulated as a semidefinite program (SDP). For a linear loss f(x,ξ) = c(x)ᵀξ + d(x), the dual of the moment problem yields sup { c(x)ᵀμ + d(x) + Tr(Σ½ZΣ½) : [ Z c(x) ; c(x)ᵀ 1 ] ⪰ 0, Z ⪰ 0 }. This SDP has dimension n+1 where n is the dimension of ξ, and can be solved in polynomial time by interior‑point methods.

If the support set Ξ is a polyhedron { ξ : Aξ ≤ b }, additional linear constraints appear in the dual, leading to a generalized SDP with linear matrix inequalities (LMIs) and linear inequalities. The resulting problem remains tractable for moderate n (up to a few hundred) using solvers such as MOSEK or SDPT3. For larger n, a first‑order method like the alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM) applied to the dual SDP scales to thousands of variables.

Extensions include (i) higher‑order moment constraints (skewness, kurtosis) which lead to larger LMIs via the moment‑SOS hierarchy; (ii) Wasserstein ambiguity sets, which admit conic reformulations that are often tighter than moment sets for the same data; (iii) φ‑divergence ambiguity sets (e.g., KL divergence) that yield convex but non‑SDP reformulations solvable by conic optimization. The choice of ambiguity set trades off conservatism and computational burden: moment sets are computationally cheapest but can be overly conservative when the true distribution has light tails; Wasserstein sets provide finite‑sample guarantees at the cost of larger conic programs.

Bilevel Optimization with Equilibrium Constraints: MPEC Reformulations and Relaxation Strategies

Bilevel programs where the lower level is a variational inequality or a Nash equilibrium can be reformulated as mathematical programs with equilibrium constraints (MPECs). The lower‑level equilibrium condition 0 ∈ F(y) + NY(y) (where NY is the normal cone to the feasible set Y) is replaced by its KKT conditions, yielding complementarity constraints 0 ≤ y ⟂ F(y) + ∇g(y)λ ≥ 0, g(y) ≤ 0, λ ≥ 0, λᵀg(y) = 0. The resulting MPEC is a single‑level nonconvex program with complementarity constraints that violate standard constraint qualifications (MFCQ fails at any feasible point).

Relaxation strategies include: (1) Scholtes relaxation — replace yᵢ(F(y)+∇g(y)λ)ᵢ = 0 by yᵢ(F(y)+∇g(y)λ)ᵢ ≤ t, t → 0; this yields a sequence of NLPs satisfying MFCQ for t > 0. (2) Exact penalty — add ρ Σ yᵢ(F(y)+∇g(y)λ)ᵢ to the objective with sufficiently large ρ; under calmness conditions the penalized problem has the same local minima. (3) Smoothing — use a smooth approximation of the complementarity function, e.g., φμ(a,b) = a+b−√((a−b)²+μ²), and solve a sequence of smooth NLPs as μ → 0. (4) Disjunctive programming — model each complementarity pair as a disjunction (yᵢ = 0 ∨ (F+∇gλ)ᵢ = 0) and apply big‑M or convex hull reformulations, leading to a mixed‑integer program that can be solved by branch‑and‑bound.

Algorithmic practice: start with a Scholtes relaxation to obtain a feasible point, then switch to an exact penalty or smoothing method for fast local convergence. For large‑scale problems (e.g., electricity market equilibrium with thousands of generators), a tailored interior‑point method that exploits the block structure of the Jacobian of F is essential. Global optimization can be pursued via spatial branch‑and‑bound on the MPEC reformulation, using convex envelopes of the complementarity constraints derived from the perspective reformulation.

Scenario Tree Construction, Reduction, and Stability in Multi‑Stage Stochastic Programming

Multi‑stage stochastic programs require a scenario tree that captures the evolution of uncertainty over time. A common construction pipeline: (1) generate a large fan of sample paths (e.g., 10⁵ paths) from the stochastic process; (2) apply a clustering algorithm (k‑means, hierarchical agglomerative) at each stage to group paths with similar histories; (3) compute conditional probabilities for each cluster to form tree nodes; (4) enforce non‑anticipativity by ensuring that scenarios sharing the same history up to stage t belong to the same node. The quality of the tree is measured by the Wasserstein distance between the discrete tree distribution and the true process distribution.

Scenario reduction (Heitsch & Römisch, 2003) removes nodes while preserving the distance bound. The forward selection algorithm iteratively deletes the node whose removal causes the smallest increase in the Wasserstein distance, recomputing probabilities of remaining nodes by redistributing the deleted node's mass to its nearest neighbor. Backward reduction starts from a large tree and merges nodes. Both algorithms guarantee that the reduced tree's distance to the original tree is bounded by the sum of deleted nodes' probabilities times their distances to the absorbing nodes.

Stability analysis quantifies how the optimal value and solution change when the tree is perturbed. For convex multi‑stage problems, the optimal value function is Lipschitz continuous with respect to the Wasserstein distance: |v(𝒫) − v(𝒫')| ≤ L · dW(𝒫,𝒫') where L is the Lipschitz constant of the cost‑to‑go functions. This bound guides the choice of tree size: to achieve ε‑optimality, the tree must satisfy dW(𝒫,𝒫*) ≤ ε/L. In practice, one constructs a sequence of trees with increasing fidelity, solves the stochastic program on each, and stops when the objective stabilizes within tolerance. Adaptive refinement — adding nodes only in regions where the cost‑to‑go gradient is large — reduces the number of scenarios dramatically compared to uniform refinement.

  • Confusing the classical L‑shaped method with its regularized variant; the latter adds a proximal term that changes the master problem structure and convergence proof.
  • Assuming that LP‑based optimality cuts are valid for integer recourse; they underestimate the true value function and can lead to infeasible master solutions.
  • Treating moment‑based ambiguity sets as equivalent to Wasserstein sets; they impose different conservatism levels and admit different reformulation classes (SDP vs. conic).
  • Applying standard NLP solvers directly to an MPEC without relaxation; constraint qualifications fail and solvers may converge to non‑stationary points.
  • Building a scenario tree by naive Monte‑Carlo sampling without clustering or moment matching; the resulting tree often violates non‑anticipativity or yields a poor approximation of the true process distribution.
  • Neglecting to recompute Wasserstein distances after each reduction step; iterative reduction with updated distances is required for theoretical guarantees.
  • Using a single fixed step size in the subgradient method for Lagrangian duals; diminishing step‑size rules are necessary for convergence to the dual optimum.

Distributionally Robust Optimization with Goodness‑of‑Fit Ambiguity Sets

Distributionally robust optimization (DRO) guards against misspecification of the probability distribution that drives uncertain parameters. Classical DRO models employ ambiguity sets defined by moment constraints or by a Wasserstein ball around an empirical distribution. An alternative, rooted in statistical hypothesis testing, constructs ambiguity sets from goodness‑of‑fit statistics such as the Kolmogorov–Smirnov, Cramér–von Mises, or Anderson–Darling distances. For a sample of size N, the set of distributions 𝒫 satisfying supₓ|F_N(x)−F(x)| ≤ ε_N (Kolmogorov–Smirnov) or ∫(F_N−F)² dF_N ≤ ε_N (Cramér–von Mises) contains the true distribution with prescribed confidence 1−α. These sets are non‑convex in the space of distribution functions but become tractable after dualization: the worst‑case expectation of a piecewise‑linear convex cost can be expressed as a finite‑dimensional convex program involving the empirical CDF and the test statistic’s critical value. The resulting reformulation typically introduces auxiliary variables for each sample point and a scalar representing the supremum deviation, leading to a linear or second‑order cone program depending on the chosen statistic. Because the critical values ε_N are known analytically or via tabulated quantiles, the modeler can directly calibrate the conservatism level without solving a separate estimation problem. Goodness‑of‑fit ambiguity sets are especially attractive when the support of the uncertainty is unbounded or when moment information is unreliable; they exploit the full empirical distribution shape while retaining polynomial‑time solvability for many loss functions. Recent extensions combine multiple test statistics to form intersection ambiguity sets, tightening the coverage guarantee at the cost of additional conic constraints. In practice, the choice of statistic influences both the size of the feasible region and the numerical conditioning of the resulting conic program; Anderson–Darling, which weights tail deviations more heavily, often yields less conservative solutions for risk‑averse objectives.

Adjustable Robust Optimization with Nonlinear Decision Rules

Adjustable robust optimization (ARO) models decisions that adapt to revealed uncertainty through decision rules mapping the uncertain parameter vector ξ to the recourse variables y(ξ). The classical affine decision rule y(ξ)=Y₀+Y₁ξ yields a tractable robust counterpart when the uncertainty set is polyhedral or ellipsoidal, but it can be overly conservative for problems with nonlinear recourse structure. Nonlinear decision rules — polynomial, piecewise‑affine, or neural‑network‑parameterized mappings — enlarge the feasible policy space and often recover near‑optimal adjustable solutions. A polynomial decision rule of degree d expresses each component of y(ξ) as a sum of monomials up to order d in ξ. Substituting such a rule into the robust constraints generates polynomial inequalities in ξ that must hold for all ξ in the uncertainty set. By invoking sum‑of‑squares (SOS) relaxations or the Lasserre hierarchy, these infinite‑dimensional constraints are approximated by a sequence of semidefinite programs whose optimal values converge to the true robust optimum as the degree increases. Piecewise‑affine rules partition the uncertainty set into polyhedral regions (e.g., via a Voronoi tessellation or a decision tree) and assign an affine rule per region; the resulting robust counterpart is a mixed‑integer linear program if the partition is fixed, or a bilevel program if the partition is optimized. Neural‑network decision rules, parameterized by weights and ReLU activations, lead to robust constraints that can be enforced via mixed‑integer linear formulations leveraging the piecewise‑linear nature of ReLU. The trade‑off is clear: higher expressive power reduces conservatism but increases computational burden, often shifting the problem from a linear or conic program to a large‑scale MILP or SDP. In applications such as multi‑stage inventory control, energy dispatch with storage, or robust portfolio rebalancing, quadratic or piecewise‑affine rules have been shown to close a substantial fraction of the gap between static robust and fully adjustable policies while remaining solvable within practical time limits.

Worked Example: Polynomial Decision Rule for a Two‑Stage Robust Production Problem
A manufacturer must decide first‑stage production quantities x ≥ 0 before demand ξ ∈ [−1,1] is realized. Second‑stage adjustment y(ξ) ≥ 0 incurs cost cᵀy(ξ) and must satisfy x + y(ξ) ≥ ξ. The uncertainty set is the interval [−1,1]. Using a quadratic decision rule y(ξ)=a + bξ + cξ², formulate the robust counterpart as a semidefinite program.
Substitute y(ξ) into the constraint: x + a + bξ + cξ² ≥ ξ ∀ ξ∈[−1,1]. Rearranged: cξ² + (b−1)ξ + (x+a) ≥ 0 ∀ ξ∈[−1,1]. A univariate quadratic is nonnegative on an interval iff its minimum on that interval is ≥ 0. The minimum occurs either at the boundaries ξ=±1 or at the vertex ξ* = (1−b)/(2c) if c>0 and ξ*∈[−1,1]. Enforcing nonnegativity at the boundaries yields two linear inequalities: c + (b−1) + (x+a) ≥ 0 and c − (b−1) + (x+a) ≥ 0. If c>0, the vertex condition adds (x+a) − (b−1)²/(4c) ≥ 0, which is equivalent to the linear matrix inequality [[c, (b−1)/2], [(b−1)/2, x+a]] ⪰ 0. The robust counterpart minimizes cᵀ(a + b·0 + c·E[ξ²]) subject to x≥0, a,b,c free, and the LMI plus boundary inequalities. This is an SDP in variables (x,a,b,c).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a robust counterpart with a polynomial decision rule, first write the constraint as a polynomial in the uncertain parameter. Then recall the standard nonnegativity certificates: for a univariate polynomial on an interval, use boundary checks plus an LMI for the vertex; for multivariate polynomials, invoke Putinar’s Positivstellensatz and the first‑order SOS relaxation. Sketch the resulting SDP blocks rather than expanding all monomials — examiners reward recognition of the certificate structure over algebraic expansion.

Cutting Planes from Lattice‑Free Sets in Mixed‑Integer Programming

Modern mixed‑integer programming (MIP) solvers rely heavily on cutting planes derived from the geometry of lattice‑free convex sets. A lattice‑free set in ℝⁿ is a convex set whose interior contains no integer point. The seminal result of Andersen, Louveaux, and Weismantel shows that any maximal lattice‑free convex set in ℝ² is either a split (two parallel hyperplanes), a triangle with exactly one integer point in the interior of each edge, or a quadrilateral with one integer point in the interior of each edge. These three families generate all facet‑defining inequalities for the integer hull of a two‑dimensional polyhedron intersected with the integer lattice. In higher dimensions, the classification is incomplete, but split cuts, triangle cuts (type‑1 and type‑2), and quadrilateral cuts remain the workhorses of commercial solvers. The separation problem — finding a violated cut from a given fractional LP solution — reduces to solving a small integer program or a convex optimization over the space of lattice‑free sets. For split cuts, separation is equivalent to solving a mixed‑integer linear program with a single additional binary variable; for triangle and quadrilateral cuts, it can be cast as a sequence of linear programs with a bounded number of integer variables. Practically, solvers generate split cuts (Gomory mixed‑integer cuts are a special case) at every node, while triangle and quadrilateral cuts are invoked selectively when the LP relaxation exhibits a two‑dimensional fractional extreme point. The strength of a cut is measured by its Euclidean distance to the fractional point; maximal lattice‑free cuts are the deepest possible for a given lattice‑free set. Recent advances include multi‑row cuts derived from intersections of several lattice‑free sets, and cuts from lattice‑free sets with non‑integral vertices (so‑called “non‑basic” cuts). Understanding the geometry of lattice‑free sets enables a modeler to anticipate which constraints will yield strong cuts and to reformulate models (e.g., by aggregating constraints) to expose favorable two‑dimensional subspaces for cut generation.

Interior Point Methods for Large‑Scale Linear Programming: Preconditioning and Iterative Solvers

Primal‑dual interior point methods (IPMs) solve linear programs by following the central path defined by the perturbed KKT conditions XSe = μe, where X and S are diagonal matrices of primal and dual slacks. At each iteration a Newton system of the form (AD²Aᵀ)Δy = rhs must be solved, where D² = X⁻¹S. For large‑scale problems with millions of variables and constraints, the normal equations matrix AD²Aᵀ becomes too large for direct Cholesky factorization. Iterative solvers — typically preconditioned conjugate gradients (PCG) or MINRES — are employed, but the conditioning of AD²Aᵀ deteriorates as μ → 0 because D² spans many orders of magnitude. Effective preconditioning is therefore critical. The most widely used preconditioner is the constraint preconditioner P = [−Θ⁻¹ Aᵀ; A 0] where Θ approximates D⁻², or the augmented system preconditioner based on a sparse Cholesky factorization of a regularized matrix A(D²+δI)Aᵀ. Another approach exploits the block structure of the KKT system: the symmetric quasi‑definite matrix [−D⁻² Aᵀ; A 0] can be factorized once per IPM iteration using a sparse LDLᵀ factorization with dynamic pivoting, and the factors are reused for multiple right‑hand sides in predictor‑corrector steps. For problems with network or block‑angular structure, specialized preconditioners such as the support‑graph preconditioner or the Schur‑complement preconditioner with low‑rank updates achieve near‑constant iteration counts. In practice, a hybrid strategy is common: use a direct solver for the first few IPM iterations when the matrix is well‑conditioned, then switch to PCG with a constraint preconditioner once the condition number exceeds a threshold. The choice of stopping tolerance for the iterative solver must be coordinated with the IPM’s centering parameter; too loose a tolerance stalls convergence, while too tight a tolerance wastes effort. Modern implementations (e.g., in commercial solvers) monitor the residual of the normal equations and adapt the preconditioner update frequency dynamically, achieving polynomial‑time complexity in theory and robust performance on instances with up to 10⁷ nonzeros.

Bilevel Optimization Algorithms for Optimistic and Pessimistic Formulations

Bilevel optimization models hierarchical decision‑making where a leader (upper level) chooses variables x anticipating the reaction of a follower (lower level) who solves a parametric optimization problem in y. The optimistic formulation assumes the follower selects, among possibly multiple optimal responses, the one most favorable to the leader; the pessimistic formulation assumes the worst‑case follower response. Both formulations are generally nonconvex and NP‑hard even when the lower level is a linear program. For the optimistic case with a convex lower‑level problem satisfying a constraint qualification, the Karush‑Kuhn‑Tucker (KKT) conditions are necessary and sufficient for optimality. Replacing the lower level by its KKT system yields a Mathematical Program with Equilibrium Constraints (MPEC). The complementarity conditions λᵢgᵢ(y)=0, λ≥0, g(y)≤0 can be handled by Scholtes relaxation, exact penalty, or smoothing, as discussed in the MPEC literature. For the pessimistic case, the leader must hedge against all optimal follower solutions, leading to a min‑max‑min structure. A common reformulation introduces an auxiliary variable representing the worst‑case lower‑level value and enforces that the follower’s optimal value equals this variable for all feasible follower optima. This can be expressed via a robust optimization model where the uncertainty set is the set of optimal follower solutions, which itself is described by the KKT conditions plus an optimality condition. Algorithmic approaches include: (1) branch‑and‑bound on the binary variables introduced by big‑M linearization of complementarity; (2) cutting‑plane methods that iteratively add violated constraints derived from the follower’s value function; (3) descent methods on a regularized value‑function reformulation, where the lower‑level value function is approximated by a piecewise‑linear convex envelope. For mixed‑integer lower levels, the optimistic bilevel problem can be tackled by enumerating the follower’s optimal integer solutions via a branch‑and‑cut framework that separates valid inequalities from the follower’s integer hull. The pessimistic mixed‑integer bilevel problem is significantly harder; a practical heuristic solves a sequence of optimistic problems with added constraints that exclude previously found follower optima, approximating the worst case. In energy market applications, the optimistic model corresponds to a generator assuming the ISO will dispatch in its favor, while the pessimistic model reflects a regulator’s worst‑case analysis. Understanding the distinction is essential for correctly interpreting bilevel results in policy‑oriented exams.

  • Confusing the optimistic and pessimistic bilevel formulations; the optimistic assumes a cooperative follower, the pessimistic assumes an adversarial one.
  • Applying KKT‑based MPEC reformulations to a lower‑level problem that lacks a constraint qualification; the resulting stationarity conditions may be invalid.
  • Treating split cuts as universally sufficient for all MIPs; higher‑dimensional lattice‑free cuts (triangle, quadrilateral) are often needed to close the integrality gap.
  • Using a fixed preconditioner throughout an interior‑point run; the scaling matrix D² changes dramatically, requiring adaptive preconditioner updates.
  • Assuming a polynomial decision rule of degree two always yields a tractable SDP; the resulting LMI size grows combinatorially with the number of uncertain parameters.
  • Neglecting the distinction between goodness‑of‑fit ambiguity sets and moment‑based sets; they lead to different conic reformulations (SOCP vs. SDP) and different conservatism profiles.
  • Overlooking the need for a regularization parameter in the Scholtes relaxation of complementarity; without it the relaxed problem may be infeasible.

These advanced topics illustrate how modern operations research blends convex analysis, integer geometry, and algorithmic engineering to solve decision problems under uncertainty and hierarchy.

Polyhedral Combinatorics: Facet-Defining Inequalities for Matching and Traveling Salesman Problems

The matching polytope of a graph G = (V, E) is the convex hull of incidence vectors of all matchings in G. Edmonds' theorem (1965) gives a complete linear description: for every vertex v ∈ V, the degree constraint ∑e∈δ(v) xe ≤ 1; for every odd subset U ⊆ V with |U| ≥ 3, the blossom inequality ∑e∈E(U) xe ≤ (|U|−1)/2; and non‑negativity xe ≥ 0. These constraints are facet‑defining for the matching polytope, and the separation problem for blossom inequalities can be solved in polynomial time via a minimum‑cut computation in an auxiliary graph (Padberg‑Rao, 1982). This yields a polynomial‑time algorithm for maximum‑weight matching via the ellipsoid method or, more practically, via a cutting‑plane algorithm that adds violated blossom inequalities on the fly.

The traveling salesman polytope (TSP) on a complete graph Kn is the convex hull of incidence vectors of Hamiltonian cycles. The classical formulation uses degree constraints ∑j≠i xij = 2 for all i, subtour elimination constraints ∑i∈S, j∉S xij ≥ 2 for all ∅ ⊂ S ⊂ V, and xij ≥ 0. Subtour elimination constraints are facet‑defining for the TSP polytope when 2 ≤ |S| ≤ n−2. Additional facet families include comb inequalities (Grötschel‑Padberg, 1979), clique‑tree inequalities, and path inequalities. Separation for subtour elimination is polynomial (minimum cut), while separation for comb inequalities is NP‑hard in general but admits efficient heuristics (e.g., shrink‑and‑cut). Modern TSP solvers (Concorde) combine cutting planes from these families with branch‑and‑cut, achieving exact solutions for instances with tens of thousands of cities.

Understanding which inequalities are facet‑defining is crucial for designing strong relaxations. A valid inequality aTx ≤ b defines a facet of a polytope P if the set of points in P satisfying it with equality contains affinely independent points whose affine hull has dimension dim(P)−1. For the matching polytope, Edmonds proved that every non‑trivial facet arises from either a degree constraint or a blossom inequality. For the TSP, the complete facet description is unknown for n ≥ 8, but the known families suffice to close the integrality gap on most practical instances. Examiners often test the ability to identify whether a given inequality is facet‑defining by checking the dimension of the face it induces.

Superadditive Duality and Cutting Planes from Lattice‑Free Sets in Integer Programming

Superadditive duality provides a unifying framework for generating cutting planes in integer linear programming. Given a pure integer program max {cTx : Ax = b, x ∈ ℤ+n}, a superadditive function F : ℝm → ℝ ∪ {−∞} satisfies F(u+v) ≥ F(u)+F(v) and F(λu) = λF(u) for λ ≥ 0. The dual problem max {F(b) : F(Aj) ≤ cj ∀j, F superadditive} yields the same optimal value as the primal when the primal is feasible and bounded (Wolsey, 1981). Every valid inequality for the integer hull can be derived from a superadditive function, and conversely, each superadditive function generates a valid inequality.

Lattice‑free convex sets give a geometric way to construct superadditive functions. A set S ⊆ ℝm is lattice‑free if its interior contains no integer point. For a maximal lattice‑free convex set S with interior point b̄ (the fractional basic solution), the intersection cut derived from S is ∑j (max{0, ajT(b̄−b)) / ψS(aj)) xj ≥ 1, where ψS is the gauge function of S. When S is a split (a set between two parallel hyperplanes), the cut reduces to a Gomory mixed‑integer cut. When S is a triangle or quadrilateral in ℝ2, the cut corresponds to a two‑row intersection cut (Andersen‑Louveaux‑Weismantel, 2007). These cuts are facet‑defining for the integer hull of the corresponding two‑row relaxation and can close a significant portion of the integrality gap.

In practice, modern MIP solvers generate split cuts, triangle cuts, and quadrilateral cuts by solving a small separation LP over the lattice‑free set. The separation problem for a given lattice‑free set S is to find a violated inequality of the form ∑ αjxj ≥ 1 with αj = max{0, ajT(b̄−b)) / ψS(aj)}. For splits, this reduces to a simple rounding procedure; for triangles and quadrilaterals, it requires solving a small convex optimization problem. The strength of these cuts depends on the choice of S; maximal lattice‑free sets yield the strongest cuts. Examiners may ask to derive the intersection cut for a given two‑row tableau and a specific lattice‑free set, testing both algebraic manipulation and geometric intuition.

Worked Example: Two‑Row Intersection Cut from a Triangle
Consider the two‑row relaxation: x₁ − 2x₂ + s₁ = 0.5 3x₁ + x₂ + s₂ = 1.2 x₁, x₂ ∈ ℤ₊, s₁, s₂ ≥ 0. The fractional basic solution is (s₁, s₂) = (0.5, 1.2). Use the maximal lattice‑free triangle with vertices (0,0), (2,0), (0,2) in the (s₁,s₂)‑space. Derive the intersection cut.
The triangle S = { (s₁,s₂) ≥ 0 : s₁ + s₂ ≤ 2 } is lattice‑free and maximal. Its gauge function ψS(u) = max{ u₁, u₂, (u₁+u₂)/2 } for u ≥ 0. The rays from b̄ = (0.5,1.2) to the nonbasic columns are a₁ = (1,3) for x₁ and a₂ = (−2,1) for x₂. Compute ψS(a₁) = max{1,3,(1+3)/2}=3. ψS(a₂) = max{−2,1,(−2+1)/2}=1 (since gauge uses positive part). The intersection cut coefficients are α₁ = max{0, a₁ᵀ(b̄−b)} / ψS(a₁). Here b = (0,0) for the homogeneous system, so a₁ᵀb̄ = 1·0.5 + 3·1.2 = 4.1. α₁ = 4.1/3 ≈ 1.367. For x₂, a₂ᵀb̄ = −2·0.5 + 1·1.2 = 0.2. α₂ = 0.2/1 = 0.2. The cut is 1.367 x₁ + 0.2 x₂ ≥ 1. Multiplying by 3 to clear denominators gives 4.1 x₁ + 0.6 x₂ ≥ 3, or equivalently 41 x₁ + 6 x₂ ≥ 30. This inequality is valid for the integer hull and cuts off the fractional solution.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When asked to generate a lattice‑free cut, first identify the dimension of the relaxation (number of rows). For one row, use the Gomory mixed‑integer cut (split). For two rows, recall the three maximal lattice‑free sets in ℝ²: splits, triangles, and quadrilaterals. Write down the gauge function for the chosen set, compute the ray directions from the tableau, and apply the formula αⱼ = max{0, aⱼᵀb̄} / ψ(aⱼ). Keep arithmetic exact (fractions) to avoid rounding errors that examiners penalise.

Distributionally Robust Optimization with Optimal Transport Ambiguity Sets: Wasserstein and Beyond

Distributionally robust optimization (DRO) hedges against ambiguity in the probability distribution of uncertain parameters by optimizing over a family of distributions (an ambiguity set). The Wasserstein ambiguity set centers on an empirical distribution P̂N = (1/N) ∑i=1N δξ̂i and includes all distributions P whose Wasserstein distance from P̂N is at most θ: 𝔹θ(P̂N) = { P ∈ 𝒫(Ξ) : 𝒲(P, P̂N) ≤ θ }. The Wasserstein distance of order 1 is 𝒲(P, Q) = infπ ∈ Π(P,Q) 𝔼π[‖ξ−ζ‖], where Π(P,Q) is the set of couplings. For a convex loss function ℓ(x, ξ), the worst‑case expectation supP ∈ 𝔹θ 𝔼P[ℓ(x, ξ)] admits a tractable reformulation as a finite convex program (Mohajerin Esfahani & Kuhn, 2018). When ℓ is piecewise linear or convex quadratic, the reformulation becomes a linear program or a second‑order cone program, respectively.

The radius θ controls conservatism. Statistical guarantees show that if θ = O(√(log(1/β)/N)) for confidence level 1−β, then the true distribution lies in the ambiguity set with probability at least 1−β, provided the support Ξ has finite diameter. This yields finite‑sample guarantees for the out‑of‑sample performance of the DRO solution. Extensions include using higher‑order Wasserstein distances (p > 1) which lead to tighter sets but more complex reformulations (often involving p‑norm cones). Another direction is to combine Wasserstein distance with moment constraints, yielding ambiguity sets of the form { P : 𝒲(P, P̂N) ≤ θ, 𝔼P[φ(ξ)] ∈ M }. These hybrid sets can reduce conservatism while preserving tractability via conic duality.

Beyond Wasserstein, optimal transport theory provides other ambiguity sets such as the Kullback‑Leibler (KL) divergence ball and the φ‑divergence balls. The KL ball { P : DKL(P‖P̂N) ≤ ρ } leads to an exponential cone reformulation for the worst‑case expectation. φ‑divergence balls (including χ², Hellinger, total variation) yield conic programs whose cone depends on the conjugate of φ. The choice of divergence affects the shape of the ambiguity set and the resulting robustness‑performance trade‑off. Examiners often test the ability to write the dual of the worst‑case expectation problem and identify the resulting cone (SOCP, SDP, exponential cone) for a given loss function and ambiguity set.

Bilevel Optimization with Multiple Followers: Nash Equilibrium and Equilibrium Constraints

Classical bilevel optimization assumes a single follower. In many applications (energy markets, toll setting, network design), multiple followers interact non‑cooperatively, leading to a Nash equilibrium at the lower level. The leader anticipates this equilibrium and optimizes accordingly, resulting in a mathematical program with equilibrium constraints (MPEC) where the lower‑level equilibrium conditions are expressed via the Karush‑Kuhn‑Tucker (KKT) systems of all followers coupled through shared constraints or coupled objective functions. If each follower i solves minyi { fi(x, yi, y−i) : gi(x, yi) ≤ 0 }, a Nash equilibrium satisfies the variational inequality VI(F, Y(x)) where F stacks the gradients ∇yifi and Y(x) is the Cartesian product of feasible sets. Under convexity and a constraint qualification, the VI is equivalent to the KKT system of a single optimization problem only if the game is a potential game; otherwise the equilibrium constraints are genuinely complementarity conditions across followers.

Reformulating the multi‑follower bilevel problem as an MPEC introduces complementarity constraints for each follower: λi ≥ 0, gi(x, yi) ≤ 0, λiTgi = 0, plus stationarity ∇yifi + ∇yigiTλi = 0. The leader's problem becomes minx,y,λ { F(x, y) : upper‑level constraints, lower‑level KKT for all i }. This is a nonconvex MPEC. Standard solution approaches include: (1) Scholtes relaxation of complementarity, (2) exact penalty methods, (3) branch‑and‑bound on binary variables that model the complementarity disjunctions, and (4) descent methods on a regularized Nikaido‑Isoda function. When followers have mixed‑integer decisions, the lower level becomes a generalized Nash equilibrium problem with integer variables, which is Σ2P-hard. Practical heuristics solve a sequence of optimistic bilevel problems with added constraints that exclude previously found equilibria, approximating the pessimistic leader's perspective.

Applications include electricity market design where the regulator (leader) sets capacity payments and generators (followers) compete in a Cournot or supply‑function equilibrium. The resulting MPEC can be solved by a tailored interior‑point method that exploits the block‑angular structure of the KKT systems. Examiners may ask to write the KKT conditions for a two‑follower Cournot game with linear demand and quadratic costs, and to identify the complementarity constraints that make the problem an MPEC. They may also test the distinction between a variational equilibrium (solution of a single VI) and a normalized Nash equilibrium (where each follower's KKT uses its own multipliers for shared constraints).

Online Learning and Bandit Algorithms for Sequential Decision Making

Online convex optimization (OCO) models sequential decision making where at each round t = 1,…,T the learner chooses xt ∈ X (a convex set), then observes a convex loss function ft and incurs loss ft(xt). The performance metric is regret RT = ∑t=1T ft(xt) − minx∈X ∑t=1T ft(x). The online gradient descent (OGD) algorithm xt+1 = ΠX(xt − ηt∇ft(xt)) achieves RT = O(√T) for Lipschitz losses with step size ηt ∝ 1/√t. Accelerated gradient methods (Nesterov) do not improve the worst‑case regret in the online setting because the lower bound Ω(√T) holds for any deterministic algorithm. However, for strongly convex losses, OGD with ηt ∝ 1/t achieves logarithmic regret O(log T).

In bandit settings, the learner only observes the scalar loss ft(xt) (bandit feedback) or even just a noisy evaluation. The multi‑armed bandit (MAB) problem with K arms and stochastic rewards is solved by Upper Confidence Bound (UCB) algorithms: choose arm at = argmaxa ( μ̂a(t) + √(2 log t / na(t) ) ), achieving regret O(∑a:Δa>0 (log T)/Δa). For adversarial bandits, EXP3 (Exponential-weight algorithm for Exploration and Exploitation) maintains a probability distribution over arms and achieves expected regret O(√(KT log K)). Contextual bandits generalize MAB by observing a context vector ct before choosing an arm; LinUCB assumes linear expected reward θaTct and achieves regret O(√T log T).

When the action set is a convex body and the learner receives bandit feedback, the problem is bandit convex optimization. The one‑point gradient estimator (Flaxman et al., 2005) samples ut uniformly on the unit sphere, plays xt + δut, and estimates ∇ft(xt) ≈ (d/δ) ft(xt+δut) ut. This yields regret O(d√T) for Lipschitz functions. For strongly convex losses, the regret improves to O(d log T). These algorithms underpin modern applications such as online portfolio selection, dynamic pricing, and hyperparameter tuning. Examiners often test the derivation of the one‑point gradient estimator's unbiasedness and the resulting regret bound, as well as the distinction between stochastic and adversarial bandit regret definitions.

  • Confusing the facet‑defining conditions for matching blossom inequalities with those for TSP comb inequalities; the former are exact for all odd sets, the latter require a handle and teeth structure.
  • Assuming every lattice‑free set yields a facet‑defining cut; only maximal lattice‑free sets guarantee facet‑defining intersection cuts for the corresponding relaxation.
  • Using a Wasserstein radius θ that does not scale with sample size N; statistical guarantees require θ = O(√(log(1/β)/N)) for confidence 1−β.
  • Treating a multi‑follower bilevel problem as a single‑follower MPEC by stacking KKT conditions without checking for shared constraints that create coupled complementarity.
  • Applying UCB regret bounds to adversarial bandit settings; UCB assumes stochastic rewards, while EXP3 is needed for adversarial guarantees.
  • Neglecting the projection step in online gradient descent when the feasible set X is not the whole space; the regret analysis relies on non‑expansiveness of the projection operator.
  • Assuming the one‑point gradient estimator in bandit convex optimization is unbiased for the true gradient; it is unbiased for the gradient of a smoothed version of the loss.

Complexity Theory and Approximation Algorithms in Operations Research

Understanding the computational complexity of optimization problems is essential for selecting solution approaches and for setting realistic expectations about solution quality. The class P contains decision problems solvable in polynomial time, while NP contains problems whose solutions can be verified in polynomial time. A problem is NP‑hard if every problem in NP can be reduced to it in polynomial time; if it also belongs to NP it is NP‑complete. Classic NP‑complete problems in operations research include the traveling salesman problem (TSP), the knapsack problem, set covering, and integer linear programming in general. For NP‑hard optimization problems, exact algorithms such as branch‑and‑bound or cutting planes may require exponential time in the worst case, motivating the study of approximation algorithms.

An approximation algorithm runs in polynomial time and guarantees a solution whose objective value is within a factor α ≥ 1 of the optimum for minimization (or 1/α for maximization). The factor α is called the approximation ratio. For example, the Christofides algorithm achieves a 3/2‑approximation for metric TSP, while the greedy set‑cover algorithm yields an Hn ≈ ln n + 1 approximation. Some problems admit a polynomial‑time approximation scheme (PTAS): for any fixed ε > 0 there is a (1 + ε)‑approximation running in time polynomial in the input size (but possibly exponential in 1/ε). The Euclidean TSP and knapsack problem have PTASs. A fully polynomial‑time approximation scheme (FPTAS) further requires the running time to be polynomial in both the input size and 1/ε; knapsack is a canonical example. Conversely, APX‑hard problems such as metric TSP (without triangle inequality) or maximum independent set do not admit a PTAS unless P = NP. The PCP theorem and subsequent hardness‑of‑approximation results establish explicit inapproximability bounds; for instance, set cover cannot be approximated within (1 − o(1)) ln n unless P = NP.

In practice, the choice between exact and approximate methods depends on instance size, required solution quality, and time limits. Mixed‑integer programming solvers exploit problem structure (e.g., total unimodularity, perfect formulations) to solve many NP‑hard instances to optimality. When exact solution is infeasible, approximation algorithms provide provable guarantees, while heuristics (metaheuristics, matheuristics) often find high‑quality solutions without guarantees. Examiners frequently test the distinction between PTAS and FPTAS, the approximation ratio of greedy algorithms for submodular maximization (1 − 1/e), and the ability to recognize when a problem’s special structure (e.g., network flow, matroid) places it in P.

Matheuristics: Hybrid Exact‑Heuristic Frameworks for Large‑Scale Problems

Matheuristics combine the rigor of mathematical programming with the flexibility of metaheuristics to tackle large‑scale mixed‑integer problems that are beyond the reach of pure exact or pure heuristic methods. The central idea is to decompose the problem into subproblems that can be solved exactly (often by a MIP solver) while a higher‑level heuristic guides the search across the solution space. Common matheuristic frameworks include local branching, relax‑and‑fix, corridor method, fix‑and‑optimize, and large neighborhood search (LNS) with exact subproblem solvers.

In local branching, a constraint is added to the MIP model that limits the Hamming distance between the current integer solution and the next one, creating a neighborhood that is explored by the MIP solver. The constraint ∑j∈S (1 − xj) + ∑j∉S xj ≤ k forces at most k binary variables to flip, where S is the set of variables fixed to 1 in the incumbent. By iteratively adjusting k and resolving, the method performs a controlled walk through the solution space. Relax‑and‑fix partitions the variables into blocks; in each iteration a block is relaxed (integrality dropped) while others are fixed to their current values, and the resulting restricted MIP is solved to optimality. This yields a sequence of improving solutions and is particularly effective for lot‑sizing and scheduling problems with a natural time decomposition.

The corridor method builds a “corridor” around a reference solution by allowing variables to vary within bounds derived from the LP relaxation or from a pool of good solutions. The restricted problem is then solved exactly, often producing a better solution that becomes the new reference. Fix‑and‑optimize is similar but fixes a subset of variables to values from a heuristic solution and reoptimizes the remaining variables. Large neighborhood search destroys part of a current solution (e.g., by unassigning a set of customers in a vehicle routing problem) and then reoptimizes the destroyed part exactly, possibly using column generation or a MIP model. The destruction and repair steps are guided by metaheuristic acceptance criteria (simulated annealing, tabu search).

Matheuristics have been successfully applied to vehicle routing, crew scheduling, supply chain design, and energy systems. Their performance hinges on the quality of the decomposition, the size of the exact subproblems, and the coordination strategy. Examiners may ask to formulate a local‑branching constraint for a given binary solution, to describe how relax‑and‑fix exploits a time‑indexed formulation, or to compare the exploration‑exploitation trade‑off in LNS versus pure branch‑and‑bound.

Worked Example: Local Branching Constraint
Given a binary vector x* = (1,0,1,1,0) for variables x1…x5, write the local‑branching constraint that allows at most k = 2 variables to change value.
The set S = {j | x*_j = 1} = {1,3,4}. The local‑branching constraint is ∑j∈S (1 − x_j) + ∑j∉S x_j ≤ k. Substituting: (1−x1)+(1−x3)+(1−x4) + x2 + x5 ≤ 2. Simplifying: 3 − x1 − x3 − x4 + x2 + x5 ≤ 2 → x2 + x5 − x1 − x3 − x4 ≤ −1. This constraint forces the next integer solution to differ from x* in at most two binary variables.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a matheuristic design, first identify a natural decomposition (time, geography, product families). Then specify the exact subproblem (MIP model) and the heuristic coordination (neighborhood definition, acceptance rule). Examiners reward clear separation of the exact and heuristic components and a justification of why the subproblem remains tractable.

Polyhedral Combinatorics: Facet‑Defining Inequalities for Classic Combinatorial Polytopes

Polyhedral combinatorics studies the convex hull of feasible integer solutions to combinatorial optimization problems. A valid inequality is facet‑defining if the set of points satisfying it at equality contains affinely independent points whose number equals the dimension of the polytope. Facet‑defining inequalities are the strongest possible linear cuts; adding all of them yields the integer hull. For the traveling salesman polytope on a complete graph Kn, the classic facet families include: (1) degree constraints ∑e∈δ(v) xe = 2 for each vertex v; (2) subtour elimination constraints ∑e∈δ(S) xe ≥ 2 for all ∅ ⊂ S ⊂ V; (3) comb inequalities with a handle H and an odd number t ≥ 3 of teeth T1,…,Tt such that each tooth intersects the handle and the teeth are pairwise disjoint outside the handle; the inequality ∑e∈δ(H) xe + ∑i=1t ∑e∈δ(T_i) xe ≥ 3t + 1 is facet‑defining for the TSP polytope when certain conditions hold. (4) clique tree and path inequalities generalize combs. For the matching polytope on a general graph, Edmonds’ theorem states that the facets are given by non‑negativity xe ≥ 0, degree constraints ∑e∈δ(v) xe ≤ 1, and odd‑set constraints ∑e∈E(S) xe ≤ (|S|−1)/2 for every odd cardinality subset S ⊆ V. All these inequalities are facet‑defining for the matching polytope.

For the knapsack polytope {x ∈ {0,1}n : ∑ ai xi ≤ b}, facet‑defining inequalities include the cover inequalities ∑i∈C xi ≤ |C|−1 for minimal covers C (sets whose total weight exceeds b but any proper subset does not). Lifting procedures (sequential, simultaneous) strengthen cover inequalities to facet‑defining ones. The cutting‑plane algorithm for integer programming iteratively separates violated facet‑defining inequalities; for TSP, the separation of subtour elimination constraints reduces to a minimum cut problem, while comb separation is NP‑hard but heuristic separation routines are effective in practice.

Understanding which inequalities are facet‑defining for a given polytope is crucial for designing strong formulations and for implementing branch‑and‑cut algorithms. Examiners often test the ability to write the odd‑set constraint for a given odd vertex set in a matching problem, to identify whether a given comb structure satisfies the handle‑tooth intersection conditions, or to explain why the subtour elimination constraints alone do not describe the TSP polytope for n ≥ 6.

Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm and Variational Quantum Methods for Combinatorial Optimization

The Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) is a variational quantum algorithm designed to find approximate solutions to combinatorial optimization problems. It prepares a parameterized quantum state |γ,β⟩ = ∏l=1p e−iβlB e−iγlC |+⟩⊗n, where C is a diagonal cost Hamiltonian encoding the objective function (e.g., C = ∑(i,j)∈E (1−ZiZj)/2 for MaxCut), B = ∑i=1n Xi is a mixing Hamiltonian, and γ, β are real parameters optimized classically. The algorithm alternates between applying the problem unitary e−iγC and the mixer unitary e−iβB for p layers. For p → ∞, QAOA can reach the exact ground state under certain conditions (e.g., adiabatic theorem), but practical implementations use small p (typically 1–5) on noisy intermediate‑scale quantum (NISQ) devices.

The Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) is a broader framework where a parameterized ansatz state is optimized to minimize the expectation value of a Hamiltonian. QAOA can be viewed as a specific VQE ansatz with a problem‑inspired structure. For combinatorial problems, the cost Hamiltonian is constructed by mapping binary variables to Pauli‑Z operators: xi → (1−Zi)/2. Constraints are handled either by penalty terms in the Hamiltonian or by designing a constraint‑preserving mixer (e.g., XY‑mixer for fixed‑Hamming‑weight subspaces). The classical optimization of parameters is typically performed with gradient‑free methods (Nelder‑Mead, COBYLA) or gradient‑based methods using parameter‑shift rules.

Performance guarantees for QAOA exist for specific problems: for MaxCut on 3‑regular graphs, p=1 QAOA achieves an approximation ratio of at least 0.6924, surpassing the best classical polynomial‑time guarantee of 0.878 (Goemans‑Williamson) only for certain instances. However, no general constant‑factor approximation guarantee is known for arbitrary graphs at fixed p. Recent work explores recursive QAOA, warm‑starting from classical solutions, and quantum‑classical hybrid branch‑and‑bound where QAOA provides lower bounds. Examiners may ask to write the cost Hamiltonian for a given Ising formulation, to explain the role of the mixer Hamiltonian, or to discuss the barren‑plateau phenomenon that hampers gradient‑based optimization for deep circuits.

Risk‑Averse Stochastic Dual Dynamic Programming with Time‑Consistent Risk Measures

Stochastic Dual Dynamic Programming (SDDP) solves multi‑stage stochastic linear programs by building piecewise‑linear approximations of the cost‑to‑go functions via Benders cuts. The risk‑neutral version minimizes expected total cost. To incorporate risk aversion, one replaces the expectation operator with a coherent risk measure ρ that satisfies monotonicity, translation invariance, positive homogeneity, and subadditivity. A risk measure is time‑consistent if the dynamic programming principle holds: ρt(X) = ρt(ρt+1(X)) for all stages t. The Conditional Value‑at‑Risk (CVaR) at level α is time‑consistent when composed via the nested formulation ρt(X) = CVaRα(X | ℱt). The Optimized Certainty Equivalent (OCE) with a disutility function u provides another time‑consistent family.

In risk‑averse SDDP, the backward pass computes cuts for the risk‑adjusted cost‑to‑go function. For a stage‑wise independent process, the risk‑averse Bellman equation becomes Vt(x) = miny∈Y(x) { ctTy + ρt( Vt+1(Aty + Btξt+1) ) }. When ρ is CVaR, the inner risk measure can be dualized, introducing auxiliary variables and additional constraints in the stage subproblem. The resulting subproblem remains a linear program (or a convex program for OCE), so Benders cuts can still be generated. The cuts are derived from the dual of the risk‑adjusted subproblem and are valid lower approximations of the risk‑averse value function.

Convergence of risk‑averse SDDP requires the risk measure to be law‑invariant and time‑consistent, and the scenario tree to satisfy certain regularity conditions. Practical implementations use cut selection and regularization (e.g., proximal terms) to stabilize the algorithm. Risk‑averse SDDP has been applied to hydro‑thermal scheduling, reservoir management, and energy storage optimization where the decision maker wishes to hedge against adverse tail events. Examiners may test the derivation of the CVaR dual representation, the formulation of the risk‑averse stage subproblem, or the distinction between time‑consistent and time‑inconsistent risk measures (e.g., VaR is not time‑consistent).

  • Confusing the approximation ratio of a PTAS (1 + ε) with that of an FPTAS (also 1 + ε but with polynomial dependence on 1/ε).
  • Assuming that every valid inequality for a combinatorial polytope is facet‑defining; only those that achieve the full dimension are facets.
  • Writing a local‑branching constraint without correctly partitioning variables into S and its complement.
  • Treating the QAOA mixer Hamiltonian as always ∑ Xi; constrained problems require tailored mixers (e.g., XY‑mixer).
  • Using a risk measure such as VaR in a multi‑stage SDDP setting without verifying time‑consistency, leading to incorrect dynamic programming recursions.
  • Believing that comb inequalities for TSP are facet‑defining for any handle and teeth; the handle must intersect each tooth and teeth must be disjoint outside the handle.
  • Neglecting the need for constraint qualification when dualizing the CVaR representation in risk‑averse SDDP subproblems.

These advanced topics deepen the theoretical foundation and practical toolkit required for modern operations research examinations.

Polyhedral Theory: Facet-Defining Inequalities and Separation Algorithms

The strength of a mixed‑integer programming formulation is governed by the polyhedron described by its linear relaxation. A valid inequality aTx ≤ b is facet‑defining for a polyhedron P if the set of points in P satisfying it at equality has dimension dim(P)−1. Facets are the maximal proper faces of P; any linear description of P must contain all facet‑defining inequalities (up to redundancy). For combinatorial problems such as the traveling salesman problem, the convex hull of feasible tours is known to be described by exponentially many facet classes: subtour elimination constraints, comb inequalities, clique‑tree inequalities, and path inequalities. The separation problem — given a fractional point x*, find a violated facet or prove none exists — is polynomial‑time equivalent to optimization over P (Grötschel‑Lovász‑Schrijver theorem). In practice, exact separation is often NP‑hard, so heuristic separation routines are used: for subtour elimination, a minimum‑cut computation on a support graph with capacities x* identifies violated constraints in O(|V|2) time; for comb inequalities, a shrink‑and‑expand heuristic searches for handles and teeth that yield violation. Modern solvers embed a portfolio of separation callbacks: Gomory mixed‑integer cuts derived from the optimal simplex tableau, mixed‑integer rounding cuts, and lifted cover cuts for knapsack structures. Lifting transforms a valid inequality for a lower‑dimensional face into a facet of the full polyhedron by sequentially strengthening coefficients. The sequence of lifting (exact vs. approximate) determines the final cut strength. Implementations typically use approximate lifting for speed, followed by a post‑processing step that attempts to recover exact facet coefficients. Understanding which inequalities are facet‑defining for a given formulation allows the modeler to add them a priori (cut‑and‑solve) or to guide the solver’s cut generation priority, dramatically reducing branch‑and‑bound tree size.

Hybrid Metaheuristics: Memetic Algorithms and Hyperheuristics

Pure metaheuristics such as genetic algorithms, simulated annealing, or tabu search explore the solution space through stochastic moves but often lack intensive local refinement. Memetic algorithms (MAs) address this by embedding a deterministic local search (the “memetic” operator) inside an evolutionary framework: each offspring undergoes a hill‑climbing or variable‑neighborhood descent before entering the population. The balance between global exploration (crossover, mutation) and local exploitation (memetic improvement) is controlled by the frequency and intensity of the local search; too frequent local search leads to premature convergence, while too sparse yields slow progress. Adaptive MAs adjust this balance online by monitoring population diversity metrics such as average Hamming distance or entropy of allele frequencies. Hyperheuristics operate at a higher level: they search over a space of heuristics (or heuristic components) rather than directly over problem solutions. A selection hyperheuristic maintains a set of low‑level heuristics (e.g., 2‑opt, 3‑opt, Or‑opt for TSP) and a learning mechanism (reinforcement learning, bandit algorithms, or choice functions) that decides which heuristic to apply at each step based on recent performance. Generation hyperheuristics evolve new heuristics by combining primitive operators via genetic programming. The key theoretical result (Burke et al., 2003) shows that a well‑designed hyperheuristic can achieve performance comparable to problem‑specific metaheuristics across a benchmark suite, demonstrating a form of “general‑purpose” intelligence. In practice, memetic algorithms dominate in combinatorial domains where strong local search exists (e.g., graph coloring, vehicle routing), while hyperheuristics excel when the problem changes dynamically or when a portfolio of problem instances must be solved with a single configurable solver. Both paradigms benefit from parallel island models: subpopulations evolve independently with occasional migration, providing both diversification and robustness to parameter settings.

Worked Example: Designing a Memetic Algorithm for the Quadratic Assignment Problem
Formulate a memetic algorithm for the quadratic assignment problem (QAP) with n facilities and n locations. Specify the representation, crossover, mutation, and the local search operator. Explain how to set the local search frequency to avoid premature convergence.
Representation: permutation π of {1,…,n} where π(i) = location assigned to facility i.
Crossover: use order‑based crossover (OX2) that preserves relative order of a randomly chosen subsequence from parent A and fills remaining positions from parent B.
Mutation: apply a swap mutation (exchange two positions) with probability pmut = 1/n.
Local search: implement a 2‑opt neighborhood on the permutation (swap two facilities) with first‑improvement descent; compute Δ cost in O(n) using incremental update of the quadratic objective.
Frequency control: apply local search to every offspring with probability pls = 0.3 initially. Monitor population diversity D = average pairwise Kendall‑tau distance. If D falls below 0.2·n(n−1)/2, reduce pls by 0.05; if D rises above 0.5·n(n−1)/2, increase pls by 0.05, keeping pls ∈ [0.1, 0.5]. This adaptive scheme maintains exploration while exploiting high‑quality regions.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks to “design a hybrid metaheuristic”, first name the high‑level paradigm (memetic or hyperheuristic), then list the three pillars: solution representation, global operators (crossover/mutation or heuristic selection), and local refinement (neighborhood, acceptance). Explicitly state a diversity‑control mechanism; examiners award marks for adaptive parameter schemes.

Quantum Annealing and Ising Formulations for Combinatorial Optimization

Quantum annealing (QA) exploits quantum tunneling to escape local minima in the energy landscape of an Ising Hamiltonian H = Σi hiσiz + Σi<j</sub> Jijσizσjz, where σz are Pauli‑Z operators with eigenvalues ±1. A combinatorial problem is mapped to an Ising model by encoding binary variables xi ∈ {0,1} as (1−σiz)/2. For the maximum cut problem, the cost Hamiltonian becomes HC = −½ Σ(i,j)∈E wij(1−σizσjz). Constraints are handled either by penalty terms (e.g., adding M(Σ xi−k)2 for cardinality constraints) or by designing a constraint‑preserving driver Hamiltonian (XY‑mixer for fixed‑Hamming‑weight subspaces). The annealing schedule interpolates between a transverse‑field driver HD = −Γ Σ σix and the problem Hamiltonian: H(t) = (1−t/T)HD + (t/T)HC. The adiabatic theorem guarantees convergence to the ground state if T scales inversely with the minimum spectral gap squared; however, for NP‑hard problems the gap closes exponentially, so practical QA runs with finite T and relies on thermal excitations and diabatic transitions. Current quantum annealers (D‑Wave Advantage) realize up to 5000 qubits with a Chimera/Pegasus topology; minor‑embedding maps logical variables to chains of physical qubits, introducing chain‑break errors that are mitigated by majority‑vote post‑processing. Classical simulators such as simulated quantum annealing (SQA) and path‑integral Monte Carlo allow benchmarking on larger instances. Performance metrics include time‑to‑solution (TTS) at a given success probability and approximation ratio relative to the best known classical heuristic. Examiners may ask to write the Ising Hamiltonian for a given quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO), to explain the role of the driver Hamiltonian, or to discuss the impact of chain length on solution quality.

Symmetry Breaking in Integer Programming: Orbital Branching and Reformulations

Symmetry in integer programs arises when the feasible set is invariant under a group of variable permutations (e.g., identical machines, interchangeable colors). Unbroken symmetry causes the branch‑and‑bound tree to explore exponentially many symmetric subtrees, dramatically increasing solution time. Static symmetry‑breaking constraints (SBCs) add linear inequalities that cut off all but one representative per orbit; a classic example is the lexicographic ordering constraint x1 ≤ x2 ≤ … ≤ xn for binary variables with full symmetric group Sn. However, SBCs can weaken the LP relaxation and may be exponentially many. Orbital branching (Ostrowski et al., 2011) dynamically breaks symmetry during the search: at a node, compute the orbit of the current fractional solution under the symmetry group; select a variable orbit and branch on the sum of variables in that orbit (e.g., Σi∈orbit xi ≤ k vs. ≥ k+1). This partitions the search space without adding constraints that cut off feasible integer points. Orbital fixing extends the idea by fixing variables that are symmetric to already fixed ones. For problems with large symmetry groups (e.g., graph coloring, bin packing), orbital branching reduces tree size by orders of magnitude. An alternative is reformulation via symmetry‑reduced models: aggregate symmetric variables into a single representative and add counting constraints (e.g., for bin packing, use a set‑covering formulation where each column represents a packing pattern, eliminating permutation symmetry entirely). The trade‑off is between model size and symmetry elimination; column generation on the reformulated model often solves the LP relaxation faster. Modern solvers (SCIP, CPLEX) incorporate automatic symmetry detection (via graph automorphism tools such as Bliss or Saucy) and offer built‑in orbital branching. Understanding when to apply static SBCs versus dynamic orbital branching is crucial: static constraints are cheap but may degrade the relaxation; orbital branching adds overhead per node but preserves relaxation strength.

Column Generation Stabilization: Dual‑Optimal Inequalities and Smooth Penalties

Column generation solves large‑scale linear programs by iteratively adding variables with negative reduced cost. The dual variables (prices) from the restricted master problem (RMP) drive the pricing subproblem. In early iterations, dual values oscillate wildly, causing the pricing problem to generate columns that are later dropped — a phenomenon known as “heading‑in effect”. Stabilization techniques tame dual oscillations. Dual‑optimal inequalities (DOIs) are valid inequalities for the dual feasible region that cut off dual solutions not optimal for the full master. For a set‑partitioning master Σj aijxj = 1, x ≥ 0, the dual is max Σi πi s.t. Σi aijπi ≤ cj. A DOI takes the form Σi∈S πi ≤ β where S is a subset of rows and β is derived from a known feasible primal solution (e.g., a heuristic). Adding DOIs to the RMP restricts the dual space, yielding more stable prices. Smooth penalty stabilization adds a proximal term to the master objective: min cTx + ½ρ‖x−xc‖22 where xc is a center point (often the previous iterate) and ρ > 0 controls step size. The dual of this regularized master is a smoothed version of the original dual, with Lipschitz‑continuous gradient, enabling accelerated dual ascent. The parameter ρ can be updated adaptively: increase ρ when dual oscillations are large, decrease when progress stalls. Interior‑point stabilization solves a sequence of barrier problems for the master, naturally providing centered dual iterates. In practice, a hybrid approach — DOIs for early iterations, followed by smooth penalty as the solution approaches optimality — yields the best trade‑off between number of pricing calls and master solve time. For vehicle routing with time windows, stabilized column generation reduces total iterations by 30‑50 % compared to vanilla column generation.

  • Assuming that every valid inequality generated by a separation routine is facet‑defining; many are only supporting hyperplanes that do not define facets.
  • Using a fixed local‑search frequency in a memetic algorithm without monitoring population diversity, leading to premature convergence.
  • Mapping a constrained combinatorial problem to an Ising Hamiltonian using only penalty terms without checking penalty magnitude, causing the ground state to violate constraints.
  • Adding static symmetry‑breaking constraints that are not valid for the integer hull, inadvertently cutting off optimal integer solutions.
  • Applying column generation without any stabilization, resulting in severe dual oscillations and an excessive number of pricing iterations.
  • Treating orbital branching as a simple variable‑fixing rule; it requires branching on the sum over an orbit, not on individual variables.
  • Confusing dual‑optimal inequalities with primal cutting planes; DOIs restrict the dual space and must be derived from a known primal feasible solution.

Matheuristics: Integrating Exact Methods with Metaheuristics for Large‑Scale Mixed‑Integer Programs

Matheuristics combine the global exploration capability of metaheuristics with the rigorous optimality guarantees of exact mathematical programming. A typical design embeds a mixed‑integer programming (MIP) solver inside a metaheuristic framework: the metaheuristic generates promising sub‑problems (e.g., by fixing a subset of binary variables), the MIP solver optimizes the reduced model to proven optimality, and the resulting high‑quality solutions guide the metaheuristic’s search. This synergy is especially effective for problems with a strong combinatorial core and a relatively easy continuous relaxation, such as capacitated vehicle routing, lot‑sizing with setup times, and network design with fixed‑charge arcs. The seminal survey by Maniezzo, Stützle, and Voß (2010) classifies matheuristics into three archetypes: (i) model‑based where the metaheuristic manipulates the mathematical model (e.g., adding cuts, relaxing constraints); (ii) solution‑based where the metaheuristic works on incumbent solutions and calls the MIP solver as a local improvement operator; and (iii) hybrid where both model and solution spaces are explored simultaneously. A practical implementation for the capacitated vehicle routing problem (CVRP) uses a set‑partitioning master solved by branch‑and‑price; a genetic algorithm maintains a population of route pools, and each offspring triggers a column‑generation run that solves the pricing problem to optimality. The best columns are injected into the master, tightening the LP bound and often closing the integrality gap within a few generations. Convergence analysis shows that if the exact sub‑solver is called infinitely often on a dense set of sub‑problems, the algorithm inherits the asymptotic optimality of the underlying MIP solver, while empirical studies report 30‑50 % reduction in time‑to‑optimality on benchmark instances with up to 1000 customers. Key design decisions include the frequency of exact calls, the size of the fixed‑variable neighborhood, and the strategy for warm‑starting the MIP solver (reusing basis, cuts, and incumbent). Modern solvers (CPLEX, Gurobi, SCIP) expose callbacks that allow seamless integration, making matheuristics a standard tool in industrial‑scale optimization.

Large Neighborhood Search and Adaptive Variable Neighborhood Decomposition

Large Neighborhood Search (LNS), introduced by Shaw (1998), destroys a substantial portion of a current solution and re‑optimizes the destroyed part using an exact or heuristic subroutine. The destruction operator can be random, guided by problem structure (e.g., removing routes with high reduced cost in vehicle routing), or learned from past iterations via a bandit model. The repair step typically solves a restricted MIP that is small enough to be solved to optimality within seconds, yielding a high‑quality neighbor. Adaptive Variable Neighborhood Decomposition (AVND) extends LNS by maintaining a portfolio of destruction‑repair neighborhoods and dynamically allocating computational effort based on observed improvement rates. At each iteration, a multi‑armed bandit (e.g., UCB1) selects a neighborhood; the selected neighborhood’s destroy‑repair cycle is executed, and the reward (objective improvement) updates the bandit statistics. This mechanism automatically balances exploration of diverse neighborhoods (e.g., route‑based, customer‑based, time‑window‑based) with exploitation of the most productive ones. Empirical results on the VRPTW (Vehicle Routing Problem with Time Windows) show that AVND reaches solutions within 0.5 % of the best known optima in a fraction of the time required by a static LNS with a single neighborhood. Theoretical work by Hansen, Mladenović, and Pérez‑Brito (2010) proves that if each neighborhood is connected (any feasible solution can be reached by a finite sequence of moves) and the bandit policy satisfies a diminishing‑exploration condition, the algorithm converges almost surely to a local optimum with respect to the union of neighborhoods. Practical tips: limit the destroyed sub‑problem to ≤ 15 % of variables to keep MIP solves fast; use warm‑starts and retain cuts across repairs; and periodically inject a complete re‑optimization of the full model to escape deep local optima.

Worked Example: Adaptive LNS for a Small VRPTW
Consider a VRPTW with 50 customers, 5 vehicles, and a time‑window width of 30 minutes. The current solution has total travel distance 1200 km. An LNS iteration destroys the routes of 8 randomly chosen customers (≈ 16 % of customers). The repair MIP (set‑partitioning formulation restricted to the destroyed customers plus all feasible routes that serve only those customers) solves in 2.3 s and yields a new solution of distance 1185 km. The bandit reward for the “random‑customer” neighborhood is +15 km. In the next iteration the bandit selects a “worst‑route” neighborhood (destroy the route with highest reduced cost) which improves the solution to 1170 km. After 20 iterations the best solution found is 1150 km. Explain why the bandit policy prefers the worst‑route neighborhood after a few iterations.
The UCB1 index for a neighborhood i after n total pulls and n_i pulls of i is ̄r_i + √(2 ln n / n_i). Initially both neighborhoods have n_i = 1, so the exploration term dominates and each is tried. After the random‑customer neighborhood yields +15 km and the worst‑route neighborhood yields +30 km, the empirical mean ̄r for worst‑route becomes larger. As n grows, the exploration term shrinks, so the index for worst‑route exceeds that of random‑customer, causing the bandit to select worst‑route more often. This matches the observed behavior: the algorithm automatically shifts effort to the more productive destruction strategy.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks to design or analyze a matheuristic, first identify the exact sub‑problem (MIP, LP, or network flow) and the metaheuristic component (genetic algorithm, tabu search, LNS). Then discuss warm‑starting, cut sharing, and the trade‑off between sub‑problem size and solution quality. For LNS/AVND, be ready to write the UCB1 formula and explain how the destruction percentage affects MIP solve time. Mention that convergence proofs require connected neighborhoods and diminishing exploration.

Info‑Gap Decision Theory for Severe Uncertainty in Optimization

Info‑Gap Decision Theory, developed by Yakov Ben‑Haim (2001), addresses decision making under severe uncertainty where probability distributions are unavailable or unreliable.
